## Response:
Thought for 1m 30s

Yes — **you can build an “AlphaProof++-lite” on a laptop**, as long as you mean:

> **LLM (policy) + proof checker (verifier) + search + a tiny self-improving loop**  
> …not “DeepMind-scale AlphaProof training on millions+ problems with huge compute.”

The good news is that a lot of the hard plumbing already exists.

The shortest path to a working prototype (laptop-friendly)
----------------------------------------------------------

### 1) Use Lean 4 as your “universe”

Lean gives you the deterministic state machine + perfect verifier.

### 2) Use LeanDojo for the “debugger/gym” interface

LeanDojo can **extract proof states/tactics/premises** and lets you **interact with Lean programmatically** (i.e., step through proof states like a debugger). [leandojo.readthedocs.io+1](https://leandojo.readthedocs.io/?utm_source=chatgpt.com)

### 3) Start with LeanCopilot as the baseline “LLM inside Lean”

LeanCopilot already does **tactic suggestions, premise selection, and automated proof search**, and it supports **local models (with/without GPU) or cloud models**. [GitHub+1](https://github.com/lean-dojo/LeanCopilot?utm_source=chatgpt.com)  
This gets you to “it proves things” fast, then you can incrementally replace parts with your own agent.

* * *

What you can realistically build on a laptop
--------------------------------------------

Think in versions:

### V0 — “Hello, verifier loop” (1–2 days)

*   Install Lean4 + mathlib
*   Use LeanCopilot to suggest tactics and get proofs working on small goals [GitHub+1](https://github.com/lean-dojo/LeanCopilot?utm_source=chatgpt.com)

### V1 — “Your own agent wrapper” (2–5 days)

*   Use LeanDojo to:
    *   read a goal state
    *   propose N tactics
    *   execute them
    *   keep the ones that advance the state [leandojo.readthedocs.io+1](https://leandojo.readthedocs.io/?utm_source=chatgpt.com)

### V2 — “Search makes it smart” (1–2 weeks)

*   Add **beam search / best-first search** over proof states.
*   Score states with a simple value heuristic (e.g., fewer goals, smaller goal complexity).

### V3 — “Compression-progress loop” (2–4 weeks)

*   Log successful proof traces (state → tactic).
*   Fine-tune a small local model (LoRA) to imitate the tactics that worked.
*   Repeat: model improves → search gets cheaper → you solve harder stuff.

That V3 loop is the **core AlphaProof idea** in miniature: _search finds proofs, training compresses them into a better policy._

* * *

What will _not_ fit on a laptop (unless you massively downscale)
----------------------------------------------------------------

*   Full AlphaProof-style RL at scale (millions of generated problems, heavy self-play, huge test-time compute).
*   Frontier-level contest problems reliably.

But you **can** absolutely build something that:

*   solves a meaningful subset of mathlib-style lemmas,
*   gets measurably better over iterations,
*   and demonstrates your “smartness = progress per compute” idea.

* * *

If you want my recommended “do this, don’t get stuck” stack
-----------------------------------------------------------

*   **Lean 4 + mathlib**
*   **LeanDojo** for programmatic stepping (the debugger/gym layer) [leandojo.readthedocs.io+1](https://leandojo.readthedocs.io/?utm_source=chatgpt.com)
*   **LeanCopilot** as baseline + reference implementation [GitHub+1](https://github.com/lean-dojo/LeanCopilot?utm_source=chatgpt.com)
*   A small local model (quantized) _or_ an API model at first (to avoid GPU headaches)
*   Beam search + caching + simple heuristics

If you want, tell me your laptop specs (Mac/Windows/Linux, RAM, GPU or not), and I’ll give you a **concrete 7-day build plan** with exact repo setup + the minimal agent loop (state → propose tactics → run → update search).

## Response:
Thought for 10m 50s

You can absolutely build an **AlphaProof++-style system** on your laptop in Cursor — meaning:

*   **Lean 4** as the deterministic “universe”
*   a **verifier-in-the-loop** agent that proposes proof steps and gets instant correctness feedback
*   **search** (so it’s not just one-shot generation)
*   **retrieval / premise selection** (so it can actually use mathlib)
*   a **self-improvement loop** (so it gets better over time)

That part is feasible.

What’s _not_ feasible is: “a laptop-only system will beat DeepMind’s AlphaProof on IMO-level problems _handily_” in the full sense. AlphaProof is explicitly an **AlphaZero-style RL + search** system trained at scale, and for the hardest problems it uses **test-time RL** that generates and learns from **millions of related variants**. That’s not laptop territory. [Google DeepMind+1](https://deepmind.google/blog/ai-solves-imo-problems-at-silver-medal-level/?utm_source=chatgpt.com)

But here’s the important twist that _does_ match your whole thesis:

### A smaller system can “beat” a larger one **on the right metric**

If you define “beat” as:

*   **proofs solved per unit compute**
*   **interactive usefulness** (how often it finishes your Lean goals)
*   **benchmark score under tight budgets**
*   or “I can run it locally and it’s good enough to replace me in mathlib work”

…then _yes_, you can plausibly build something that feels “unfairly strong for its size” by leaning into **retrieval + search + distillation + curriculum**.

Below is a concrete plan that’s as “go all out” as you can reasonably get without having a research lab’s compute.

* * *

The goal you should actually target
-----------------------------------

“Best mathematician in the world” is too fuzzy. In the AlphaProof-ish world, you can make it sharp.

Pick **two** success metrics:

1.  **Formal theorem proving success** on established benchmarks:
    *   **miniF2F** (Lean benchmark of olympiad-style problems). [GitHub+1](https://github.com/openai/miniF2F?utm_source=chatgpt.com)
    *   **PutnamBench** (Lean 4 subset of competition problems; much harder / broader). [trishullab.github.io+2GitHub+2](https://trishullab.github.io/PutnamBench/?utm_source=chatgpt.com)
2.  **Efficiency metric**:
    *   pass@1 under a strict node/time budget
    *   or “average wall-clock per solved theorem”
    *   or “% of mathlib goals solved with <N prover calls”

This matches your definition of intelligence: _predict/progress per fixed compute_.

* * *

The stack you’ll build in Cursor
--------------------------------

This is the minimal “AlphaProof++ architecture” that actually compounds:

### Layer 1 — Universe + Verifier: Lean 4 + mathlib

Lean is your deterministic environment; proof checking is the ground truth.

### Layer 2 — Gym/Debugger interface: LeanDojo or LeanDojo-v2

You want programmatic:

*   observe proof state
*   submit tactic
*   get next state or failure  
    LeanDojo is built for exactly this. [leandojo.readthedocs.io+2Lean Dojo+2](https://leandojo.readthedocs.io/?utm_source=chatgpt.com)  
    LeanDojo-v2 tries to make it end-to-end: data extraction + dataset mgmt + training + inference integration. [PyPI+1](https://pypi.org/project/lean-dojo-v2/?utm_source=chatgpt.com)

### Layer 3 — Policy model: a Lean-specialized open model

Your best “laptop-realistic” starting point right now is **DeepSeek-Prover-V2-7B**, explicitly trained for **Lean 4 theorem proving**, and released as open weights. [Hugging Face+1](https://huggingface.co/deepseek-ai/DeepSeek-Prover-V2-7B)  
(DeepSeek also released a 671B version with very strong reported benchmark results, but that’s “cloud teacher” territory, not “laptop runner.”) [Hugging Face+1](https://huggingface.co/deepseek-ai/DeepSeek-Prover-V2-7B)

### Layer 4 — Retrieval: premise selection from mathlib

This is non-negotiable if you want real power on mathlib-scale libraries. LeanDojo/ReProver showed retrieval materially improves theorem proving by selecting relevant premises from large libraries. [arXiv+1](https://arxiv.org/abs/2306.15626?utm_source=chatgpt.com)

### Layer 5 — Search: best-first / MCTS over proof states

This is where you “pay compute once” and then later compress it into the model.  
There are also papers showing search-method choice alone can significantly improve results with the same policy model (e.g., MCTS variants). [ICLR Proceedings](https://proceedings.iclr.cc/paper_files/paper/2025/file/69dbaf03f7f37a7ccad9ccc92875a44d-Paper-Conference.pdf?utm_source=chatgpt.com)

### Layer 6 — Self-improvement: distill search into the policy

This is the compounding loop:

*   search finds proofs (expensive)
*   you train the model on them (compression)
*   future search becomes cheaper / succeeds more often

This is the essence of “AlphaZero-style” progress, just in Lean. [Google DeepMind+1](https://deepmind.google/blog/ai-solves-imo-problems-at-silver-medal-level/?utm_source=chatgpt.com)

* * *

The build plan (phased, but no fluff)
-------------------------------------

### Phase A — Get a working prover end-to-end

**Deliverable:** Given a Lean theorem statement, your system either returns a verified proof or fails cleanly.

**Implementation pieces:**

1.  **LeanDojo-v2 project scaffold**
    *   Use it to run Lean step-by-step and to extract proof states/premises. [PyPI+1](https://pypi.org/project/lean-dojo-v2/?utm_source=chatgpt.com)
2.  **Model runner**
    *   Start with DeepSeek-Prover-V2-7B. [Hugging Face+1](https://huggingface.co/deepseek-ai/DeepSeek-Prover-V2-7B)
    *   Run locally if you can (quantized), or run in the cloud and call it via HTTP.
3.  **Tactic proposal format**
    *   Input: (goal state + local context + retrieved premises)
    *   Output: a list of tactic candidates (strings)
4.  **Verifier-in-the-loop**
    *   For each candidate tactic: try it in Lean and keep only those that advance (or solve).

If you want an “instant gratification” baseline inside Lean itself, LeanCopilot exists and already does LLM tactic suggestion + checking. It can run local or cloud models. [GitHub+2Lean Dojo+2](https://github.com/lean-dojo/LeanCopilot?utm_source=chatgpt.com)  
Even if you don’t use it long-term, it’s a reference implementation for the UX and tactic-checking loop.

* * *

### Phase B — Add retrieval (premise selection) properly

**Deliverable:** Premises are retrieved from accessible mathlib context and injected into the model prompt.

Two pragmatic options:

**Option 1: Use ReProver-style retrieval**  
ReProver is open-source and specifically designed as a retrieval-augmented prover on top of LeanDojo. [GitHub+1](https://github.com/lean-dojo/ReProver?utm_source=chatgpt.com)

**Option 2: Roll your own retrieval**

*   Extract (theorem statement, name, type, docstring-ish text) for mathlib lemmas
*   Embed them (any embedding model)
*   At runtime: retrieve top-K and add them to the prompt

Either way, retrieval is how you turn a “text model” into a “math library user.”

* * *

### Phase C — Add real search (this is the “small model feels huge” step)

**Deliverable:** Your prover solves more theorems by exploring a tree, not just one-shot sampling.

Start simple:

*   **beam search** over proof states
*   keep top B states by a heuristic score
*   expand each by generating K tactics

Then upgrade:

*   **MCTS / best-first** with a learned value function (“how close am I to a proof?”)
*   “portfolio tactics” first: try strong deterministic Lean tactics before calling the LLM (this often saves huge compute)

If you want a specific direction for “search that punches above weight,” look at the idea class of “better exploration efficiency” in theorem-proving MCTS variants (e.g., CARTS claims improved results under fixed budgets). [ICLR Proceedings](https://proceedings.iclr.cc/paper_files/paper/2025/file/69dbaf03f7f37a7ccad9ccc92875a44d-Paper-Conference.pdf?utm_source=chatgpt.com)

* * *

### Phase D — Add the self-improvement loop (the “++”)

**Deliverable:** After each run, your system gets measurably better on a held-out set.

The cleanest laptop-friendly loop is **expert iteration / distillation**:

1.  Run prover with search on a batch of theorems
2.  Save successful traces:
    *   (state, retrieved premises) → chosen tactic
3.  Fine-tune the policy with LoRA / PEFT on those traces
4.  Repeat

This is exactly your “compression-progress” story:

*   search = expensive exploration
*   finetune = compress into fast intuition

LeanDojo-v2 explicitly advertises support for training pipelines (including parameter-efficient finetuning). [PyPI+1](https://pypi.org/project/lean-dojo-v2/?utm_source=chatgpt.com)

To keep it from “only learning easy stuff,” you need curriculum:

*   sample theorems around your frontier (not too easy, not impossible)  
    LeanAgent is explicitly about lifelong learning / curriculum / expanding domains in Lean. [GitHub+2arXiv+2](https://github.com/lean-dojo/LeanAgent?utm_source=chatgpt.com)

* * *

### Phase E — Scale data without human ceiling

This is where you go from “cool toy” to “serious machine.”

You have two routes:

**Route 1: Mine mathlib harder**

*   Solve more theorems
*   Find alternate proofs
*   Distill them

There’s even work like LeanNavigator that generated **millions** of Lean theorems/proofs by exploring proof-state graphs in Mathlib4, arguing data scale is a major limiter. [arXiv+1](https://arxiv.org/abs/2503.04772?utm_source=chatgpt.com)

**Route 2: Self-generate conjectures**

*   Generate candidate conjectures
*   Try to prove/disprove
*   Keep the ones that are nontrivial + solvable  
    This exists as research too (e.g., LeanConjecturer). [arXiv](https://arxiv.org/html/2506.22005v1?utm_source=chatgpt.com)

This is the closest you can get to your “math survival ecosystem” idea.

* * *

The “beat AlphaProof while smaller” strategy that could actually work
---------------------------------------------------------------------

If you want a _real_ shot at “smaller but stronger in practice,” you don’t do it by vibes. You do it by **teacher → student distillation** plus better search.

### Teacher

Use a very strong prover (cloud):

*   DeepSeek-Prover-V2-671B as a teacher (or other frontier models)  
    DeepSeek’s model card/paper reports very strong results for the large version on benchmarks (e.g., miniF2F test). [Hugging Face+1](https://huggingface.co/deepseek-ai/DeepSeek-Prover-V2-7B)

### Student

Your laptop-runner:

*   plus your retrieval + search wrapper

### Distill what matters

*   Don’t distill “final proof only”
*   Distill **state → tactic distributions**, and **subgoal decomposition**

That’s how small models become “unnaturally competent.”

### Reality check

AlphaProof’s hardest-problem trick is _test-time RL with millions of variants_. [PubMed](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/41225005/?utm_source=chatgpt.com)  
You’re not doing that on a laptop in full generality.  
But you _can_ approximate the spirit: **test-time adaptation on a few thousand variants** + caching + distillation.

---------------------------------------------

Here’s a layout that won’t collapse under complexity:

Maybe we need a fundamentally different architecture than transformers if we’re going to compete with alpha proof … and make it run on the laptop.. maybe we need a new compression outlook and insights? Based on this convo can you think of something that might be novel and revolutionary rooted in what we discussed?

## Response:
Reasoned for 9m 41s

I think you’re onto something: **if your goal is “AlphaProof‑level results _but laptop‑runnable_,” the best bet is not “a slightly different transformer,” it’s a different _place to store intelligence_.**

Transformers store most of their competence in _weights_. AlphaProof‑style systems store a lot of competence in **(search + verifier loops + generated proof traces)**, and then compress it back into a policy. On a laptop, the move is to push even harder in that direction:

> **Make the “intelligence” live in a growing, verified _codebase of reusable proof programs_, not in a giant network.**  
> The network becomes a small router/planner.

Below are a few “novel but coherent” architectures rooted directly in what we discussed (compression-progress under fixed compute, verifier‑loops, distillation).

* * *

Idea 1: A Proof Compiler that turns search into executable macros
-----------------------------------------------------------------

### The insight

If intelligence is **compression of problem‑space traversal**, then in theorem proving the most literal compression is:

*   take an expensive proof search trace
*   compress it into a **reusable macro** (a new tactic / proof program)
*   store it in a library
*   next time, don’t “think” — just call the macro

This is like moving from “interpreting code” to **compiling** it.

### What “AlphaProof++ on a laptop” becomes

A small model (even 1–7B) doesn’t need to be a genius if it can do:

1.  **recognize which macro applies** to a goal state
2.  **fill in a few parameters** (which lemma instance, which variable, which rewrite)

The heavy intelligence accumulates as a **verified macro library**.

### Why this could be “revolutionary”

This shifts the scaling axis from “more parameters” to “more compiled skills.”  
It’s basically: **the agent writes its own mathlib of tactics**, driven by MDL pressure.

### The compression-progress objective

Keep only macros that _reduce future cost_:

$$
\text{utility(macro)} \approx \Delta(\text{avg search nodes}) - \lambda \cdot \text{macro size}
$$

So your system literally optimizes **description length of solving**, not just success.

### Why it’s laptop-friendly

Macros are code. Code is cheap to execute.  
Over time, the system becomes less like “LM inference” and more like “calling optimized subroutines.”

* * *

Idea 2: Delta‑State Reasoning instead of full-context Transformers
------------------------------------------------------------------

### The insight

In Lean, each tactic usually makes a **small edit** to the proof state:

*   goals shrink
*   context changes slightly
*   one lemma gets introduced
*   one subgoal splits

A transformer re-processes the entire context each time → expensive.

So build a model that keeps a persistent internal “proof state embedding” and updates it _incrementally_:

> **State\_t+1 = Update(State\_t, Δ)**

Where Δ is the _delta_ (what changed), not the whole state.

### Architecture sketch

*   Represent proof state as a **typed AST/graph** (Lean expressions are trees with types)
*   Maintain a **hashed DAG** of subexpressions (so identical subtrees reuse representations)
*   Use a **graph/recurrent updater** that only recomputes embeddings for changed nodes
*   The policy head proposes tactics based on this persistent embedding

### Why it could beat a transformer per compute

Because your compute becomes proportional to “how much changed,” not “how big the context is.”

This matches your “smartness per fixed compute” definition: the system spends compute _only where the frontier moved_.

### Why it’s novel (and plausible)

Most theorem-proving LMs treat proof state like text.  
But proof states are _structured programs_. Incremental compilation is exactly what compilers do.

* * *

Idea 3: “MDL‑Lemma Economy” — learning _what to remember_ as the intelligence
-----------------------------------------------------------------------------

### The insight

A big part of mathematicians being “smart” isn’t raw deduction; it’s having the right lemmas and abstractions cached in memory. In Lean, you can make that explicit:

*   the agent invents / discovers lemmas (or “convenience theorems”)
*   but only keeps the ones that reduce global description length of future proofs

### What makes it different from “just store everything”

A naive system hoards lemmas and slows down retrieval/search.

An MDL lemma economy forces _taste_:

*   a lemma is “good” if it unlocks many proofs cheaply
*   a lemma is “bad” if it’s a one-off or bloats retrieval

This is **compression as an objective**, not an emergent property.

### Why this is a real “superintelligence lever”

Humans are limited by memory and social coordination.  
A machine can maintain and optimize a _global abstraction layer_ far beyond a person.

On a laptop, you can’t brute-force as hard — so you need _better abstractions faster_. This is directly aligned.

* * *

Put the three together: The Proof OS
------------------------------------

If I had to name the “revolutionary” system implied by our convo, it’s this:

### **A Proof Operating System**

*   **Kernel:** Lean verifier (truth)
*   **Scheduler:** search/planning under compute budget (exploration)
*   **Filesystem:** a growing library of verified macros/lemmas (compiled intelligence)
*   **Index:** retrieval over lemmas/macros (fast access)
*   **Userland:** a small model that routes, parameterizes, and proposes deltas (cheap inference)
*   **Garbage collector:** MDL pruning (keeps taste; avoids bloat)

That’s a very different worldview than “bigger transformer.”

* * *

Reality check: can this “beat AlphaProof handily” on a laptop?
--------------------------------------------------------------

On _IMO‑style_ benchmarks: probably not “handily,” because AlphaProof’s edge comes from massive search and training scale.

But on your _actual_ target—**useful, strong formal math automation under tight compute**—this kind of system could plausibly outperform a much bigger “pure LM” baseline and feel absurdly capable for its size, because:

*   it amortizes search into compiled macros,
*   it stops reprocessing context,
*   and it optimizes abstraction selection explicitly.

That’s exactly the kind of “small but smarter per compute” advantage you’ve been describing the whole time.

* * *

If you want to push this into a concrete design decision
--------------------------------------------------------

Pick one “revolutionary bet” to implement first (most leverage, easiest to prototype):

1.  **Macro compiler** (fastest path to compounding on a laptop)
2.  **Delta-state model** (harder engineering, big payoff)
3.  **MDL-lemma economy** (conceptually pure, medium difficulty)

If you tell me which one you’re most excited about, I’ll outline a _minimal viable prototype_ for it that you can actually build in Cursor without it turning into a year-long research project.

## Response:
Thought for 9m 35s

Yeah — **delta‑state is plausibly the biggest payoff** _if_ you can pull it off, because it attacks the real bottleneck on a laptop:

*   Transformers are expensive mainly because they keep **re‑encoding the whole proof state** over and over.
*   In Lean, each tactic usually changes **a small part** of the state.
*   So if you can make compute scale with **“what changed”** instead of **“how big the whole state is,”** you can run a much smaller “brain” and still look strong.

The way to think about it (as a software engineer) is:

> **Build a compiler / incremental build system for proof states.**  
> Cache representations of subexpressions; when the state changes, only rebuild the affected nodes.  
> Then have a small policy select the next move.

Below is an architecture you can actually build in Cursor without being a PhD, by starting with a “string‑delta MVP” and then upgrading to “true AST‑delta.”

* * *

What “delta‑state model” means in one sentence
==============================================

Instead of feeding the full proof state into a big model every step, you maintain a **persistent internal representation** of the proof state and update it via the **diff** after each tactic.

Think incremental compilation:

*   old state → apply change → reuse old build artifacts → rebuild only what’s invalidated.

* * *

The full architecture
=====================

The big picture
---------------

```
Lean verifier (truth)
      ↑
  Apply tactic
      ↑
Search / planner  ←── uses a cheap "state embedding" + cheap "policy"
      ↑
Delta-state encoder  ←── incremental cache over state substructures
      ↑
Proof state (goals + context) from Lean
```

You’ll implement 5 components:

1.  **State extractor** (gets goals/context from Lean)
2.  **Canonicalizer + hashing** (turns states into stable pieces + hashes)
3.  **Embedding cache** (store embeddings keyed by hash)
4.  **Incremental encoder** (recompute only changed pieces)
5.  **Policy + search** (choose tactics; verifier checks)

* * *

Start with the _MVP_ version (no Lean AST needed)
=================================================

This is the one I recommend you build first because it’s _doable_ and still captures the delta idea.

MVP representation: “proof state as blocks”
-------------------------------------------

Parse the Lean goal state into blocks like:

*   each hypothesis line = a block
*   target goal line = a block
*   each goal = a list of blocks

Then:

*   hash each block string (e.g., SHA1)
*   cache an embedding for each block hash
*   state embedding = pooled embedding over block embeddings

### Why this works

When you run a tactic:

*   most hypotheses stay unchanged
*   only a few blocks change
*   you reuse cached embeddings for the unchanged blocks

That alone can cut “encoding work” dramatically and lets you use smaller models.

MVP encoder (cheap)
-------------------

You do **not** need a transformer here.

Use something like:

*   tokenize block into symbols (split on whitespace + punctuation)
*   embed tokens with a small lookup table
*   pool (mean / attention)
*   pass through an MLP to get block embedding

It’s basically a “fast bag‑of‑tokens + MLP” encoder.

MVP policy (structured, laptop‑friendly)
----------------------------------------

Don’t generate arbitrary Lean text at first. Use a fixed action set:

*   `intro`, `simp`, `aesop`, `cases`, `constructor`, `apply`, `exact`, `rw`, `linarith`, `ring`, `nlinarith`, `omega`, etc.
*   for `apply/exact/rw`: choose a lemma from retrieval

So the policy outputs:

*   a tactic template (classification)
*   optionally a lemma ID (classification over retrieved candidates)

This is much smaller than free‑form tactic generation.

MVP retrieval (non‑negotiable)
------------------------------

Maintain a searchable lemma DB (from mathlib or a smaller subset). For now:

*   embed lemma statements with the same cheap encoder
*   retrieve top‑K by cosine similarity with the goal embedding

Now your small policy can “look smart” because it can point to the right lemma.

* * *

Then upgrade to the “real” delta-state model (AST / DAG)
========================================================

Once MVP works, you’ll feel the limitations:

*   string parsing is fragile
*   block hashing misses deeper structural reuse
*   similarity is noisy

So you upgrade the representation.

Real representation: expression DAG with hash-consing
-----------------------------------------------------

Lean goals are expressions (trees). Build an internal DAG:

*   Each node is (node\_type, children, symbol)
*   Compute a **structural hash** bottom-up  
    `hash(node) = H(type, symbol, hash(child1), hash(child2), ...)`

Intern identical nodes (“hash‑consing”): if two subtrees have the same hash, they are the same node in your store.

### Delta update in the DAG world

When Lean gives you a new goal state:

*   build hashes for its AST
*   for each hash:
    *   if already in cache: reuse embedding
    *   else: compute embedding from children embeddings and store it

So the cost is proportional to **new subtrees introduced**, not the size of the whole state.

Real encoder: recursive / graph network (fast, local)
-----------------------------------------------------

You can do this with a small “tree network”:

*   Embedding for leaf symbols/constants: lookup table
*   For node types: a small MLP that combines children embeddings

Example:

*   `app(f, x)` → `MLP_app([emb(f), emb(x)])`
*   `forall(x : T), body` → `MLP_forall([emb(T), emb(body)])`
*   etc.

This is extremely laptop-friendly compared to a transformer because:

*   computation is local
*   caching is natural
*   no quadratic attention

State embedding
---------------

A proof state is multiple goals + context:

*   each goal gets an embedding (from the target expr + context exprs)
*   pool goals with a small attention module or mean pooling
*   that’s your state vector for the policy/search

* * *

The search wrapper (this is where you get “AlphaProof vibes”)
=============================================================

Even with a small policy, you get big gains by exploring:

*   Maintain a priority queue of proof states
*   Expand the best state by sampling top‑N actions from the policy
*   Apply them in Lean; keep resulting states that make progress
*   Stop when solved or budget exhausted

A simple scoring function that works surprisingly well:

*   fewer remaining goals = better
*   smaller goal AST = better
*   fewer metavariables = better
*   plus a learned value head later

This lets a small model punch above its size.

* * *

The self-improvement loop (compression-progress made real)
==========================================================

This is your “intelligence = compression of traversal” in code:

1.  Use search to solve theorems (expensive).
2.  Log trajectories: `(state_rep) -> (chosen tactic + lemma)` at each step.
3.  Train the policy to imitate those decisions (cheap).
4.  Repeat.

That’s literally: **search explores; training compresses.**

On a laptop you can:

*   train small networks locally, or
*   do periodic training on a rented GPU and pull weights back down for inference

* * *

What you should actually build first in Cursor
==============================================

If you do _only one thing first_, do this:

“DeltaCache Prover” (string/block MVP)
--------------------------------------

**Goal:** prove a subset of easy Lean theorems with:

*   verifier loop
*   block‑hash cache
*   structured tactics
*   retrieval
*   best‑first search

### Repo skeleton

```
alphaproofpp/
  lean/                # Lean project (mathlib deps)
  prover/
    env.py             # run Lean, apply tactic, get goal text
    parse.py           # split goal state into blocks
    cache.py           # hash -> embedding cache
    encoder.py         # cheap block encoder (torch)
    retrieve.py        # lemma DB + top-K retrieval
    policy.py          # pick tactic + lemma
    search.py          # best-first / beam search
    train.py           # imitation training from traces
  data/
    lemmas.jsonl       # extracted lemma statements
    traces.jsonl       # collected proof traces
```

Once that works, you’ll have the confidence + plumbing to attempt the AST‑DAG upgrade.

* * *

The honest part: why you feel overwhelmed
=========================================

Because you’re describing something that’s genuinely “researchy,” but the trick is:

**You don’t build “the revolutionary thing” first.  
You build a tiny end-to-end loop where the verifier is real.**

Lean is perfect for that because the system tells you **immediately** what works.

Your background (software engineer + EE/math) is actually a good fit because this project is 70%:

*   building a robust environment wrapper,
*   caching/incremental computation,
*   retrieval/search engineering,
*   and only 30% “mysterious ML.”

* * *

If you want, I’ll help you turn this into concrete Cursor tickets
=================================================================

Without asking you for specs, here are the next three “tickets” I’d write:

1.  **Ticket 1: Lean stepper**
    *   Run one theorem.
    *   Apply a tactic string.
    *   Return either “success + new state text” or “failure”.
2.  **Ticket 2: Block parser + cache**
    *   Parse the goal text into blocks.
    *   Hash blocks.
    *   Cache and reuse embeddings.
3.  **Ticket 3: Retrieval + structured tactics**
    *   Build lemma DB.
    *   Retrieve top‑K lemmas given a goal.
    *   Try `apply`/`exact`/`simp` with those lemmas under a small search loop.

If you say “yes, let’s do Ticket 1 now,” I’ll write the exact minimal code architecture (functions/classes, method signatures, and the control loop) so you can paste it straight into Cursor and start wiring it up.

## Response:
Thought for 8m 56s

You’re basically right — **the first “real” step (after you can _extract_ the proof state from Lean)** is to turn Lean’s expressions into a representation your agent can use cheaply and repeatedly. But the _ideal_ path for the delta‑state approach is **not** “take the pretty‑printed goal string and run a generic embedding model.”

The ideal embedding architecture is:

> **A typed, hash‑consed expression‑DAG encoder with incremental caching**, plus a **goal‑conditioned aggregator** over hypotheses/goals.

It’s more like building an **incremental compiler** than using “embeddings as a service.”

Below is what I’d build if you gave me the ultimate green light, with the explicit goal: **maximum capability per unit compute on a laptop**.

* * *

Step 0 you can’t skip: embed Lean’s _kernel AST_, not text
----------------------------------------------------------

Lean goals are not “text.” They are **typed lambda‑calculus expressions** with binders, applications, universes, metavariables, etc.

If you embed the pretty-printed string, you throw away:

*   alpha‑equivalence invariance (variable names don’t matter)
*   binder structure (∀, λ)
*   type info (which is _huge_ in Lean)
*   shared subterms (which delta‑state lives on)

So the ideal is: export `Lean.Expr` (and each local decl/type) into a stable JSON/S‑expression format.

Once you have that, embeddings become a real “compiler pipeline.”

* * *

The ideal embedding architecture: HCTE-DAG
------------------------------------------

**HCTE = Hash‑Consed Typed Expression encoder**

### Core design principles

1.  **Structure-aware**: operates on the AST/DAG, not token strings
2.  **Type-aware**: every term is tied to its type (Lean is typed; use it)
3.  **Incremental**: compute only embeddings for **new subtrees** after each tactic
4.  **Composable**: node embedding = function(child embeddings)
5.  **Cacheable**: node embedding keyed by a deterministic structural hash

That’s delta‑state in one sentence.

* * *

1) Canonical form (so caching works)
------------------------------------

You want two expressions that are “the same up to renaming” to hash the same.

Do this:

*   represent bound variables with **De Bruijn indices** (Lean already does internally)
*   strip user variable names
*   normalize trivial syntactic noise (e.g., consistent representation of applications)

Optional (later): light beta-reduction / whnf normalization, but be careful—full normalization can be expensive and can destroy sharing.

* * *

2) Hash‑consed DAG (the big payoff)
-----------------------------------

Every expression node gets a **structural hash**:

```
hash(node) = H(node_kind, symbol_id, hash(child1), hash(child2), ...)
```

Maintain a global `NodeStore`:

*   `hash -> Node(kind, symbol_id, child_hashes, embedding)`
*   if a node hash is already known, reuse it
*   if new, compute embedding once and store

**Delta‑state effect:** after each tactic, most of the goal/context is the same, so >90% of nodes reuse cached embeddings.

* * *

3) The embedding model: small “typed tree network,” not a transformer
---------------------------------------------------------------------

This is the key architectural bet.

### Node kinds you’ll handle (Lean-ish)

*   `Const(name, universe_levels)`
*   `Var(idx)` / `BVar(idx)` (bound vars)
*   `App(f, x)`
*   `Lam(type, body)`
*   `Forall/Pi(type, body)`
*   `Let(type, value, body)`
*   `Sort(u)`
*   `Lit(n)`
*   `MVar(id)` (metavariables / goals)

### Learned parameters

*   small lookup embeddings for:
    *   constant IDs (or name subtokens)
    *   node kinds
    *   binder depth bucket (optional)
*   small MLPs per constructor:
    *   `MLP_app`, `MLP_lam`, `MLP_pi`, `MLP_let`, …

### Example composition rules

Let embedding dimension be `d = 256` (laptop sweet spot).

*   **Const**
    *   `e = E_const[name] + E_univ[level_signature]`
*   **BVar**
    *   `e = E_bvar[bucket(idx)] + E_depth[bucket(depth)]`
*   **App**
    *   `e = MLP_app(concat(e_f, e_x))`
*   **Pi**
    *   `e = MLP_pi(concat(e_type, e_body))`
*   **Lam**
    *   `e = MLP_lam(concat(e_type, e_body))`

### Make it type-aware (this matters a lot)

The _same syntax_ can mean different things depending on types. You want types in the representation without making it slow.

Best practical compromise:

*   For each **top-level object** you care about (goal target, each hypothesis type), compute:
    *   `e_term = encode(expr)`
    *   `e_type = encode(type_of_expr)` (or hypothesis type already is the “type”)
*   combine:
    *   `e = MLP_termtype(concat(e_term, e_type))`

You do **not** need to infer types for every internal node at first. Start with top-level types and add more later if needed.

* * *

4) From expression embeddings to _proof-state_ embeddings
---------------------------------------------------------

A proof state isn’t one expr; it’s:

*   multiple goals
*   local context (hypotheses)
*   maybe history features

### Goal-conditioned context aggregation (cheap and effective)

Compute:

*   `g = emb(goal_target_expr)`
*   `h_i = emb(hypothesis_type_i)` for i in hypotheses

Then aggregate hypotheses using the goal as query:

*   `α_i = softmax( q(g) · k(h_i) )`
*   `ctx = Σ α_i · v(h_i)`

Finally:

*   `state = MLP_state(concat(g, ctx, scalar_features))`

Scalar features can include:

*   number of goals
*   number of hypotheses
*   step count / depth / remaining budget

This is important: it gives you a **permutation-invariant** way to handle context, while still letting the model “look at what matters.”

* * *

5) You actually need _two_ embeddings (same encoder, two heads)
---------------------------------------------------------------

To avoid “trap of using what exists,” you should explicitly design for the two real uses:

### A) Retrieval embedding (bi-encoder)

You need a stable vector for:

*   goal → nearest relevant lemmas

So use:

*   `retrieval_vec = W_ret · state`

And precompute:

*   `lemma_vec = W_lemma · emb(lemma_statement_expr)`

This powers a FAISS index locally.

### B) Policy/value embedding

You need a vector for:

*   choose tactic template
*   choose lemma among retrieved
*   estimate “promisingness” (value)

So use:

*   `policy_vec = W_pol · state`
*   `value = head_value(state)`

Same base encoder, different heads.

* * *

Why this is better than OpenAI embeddings / generic text embeddings
-------------------------------------------------------------------

Because it bakes in the properties you care about:

*   **Delta-state**: caching by structural hash makes cost ~ “new subterms,” not “entire context length”
*   **Correct invariances**: alpha‑equivalence and binder structure come for free
*   **Type sensitivity**: Lean is typed; generic embeddings throw this away
*   **Composable**: the model learns reusable substructure (exactly your compression thesis)
*   **Laptop-friendly**: MLPs + caching + no quadratic attention

A transformer can be _powerful_, but for your specific constraint (“small but scary”), this DAG encoder is the most “unfair advantage per FLOP” I can think of.

* * *

Training objective for the embedding (so it becomes meaningful)
---------------------------------------------------------------

You don’t want “embeddings that cluster similar text.” You want embeddings that make proofs cheaper.

So train with **two supervised signals from proof traces**:

1.  **Lemma relevance contrastive loss**
    *   positive: lemma actually used in the next step
*   negatives: random / in-batch / retrieved-but-not-used
*   goal: make `(state, needed_lemma)` close in vector space
    2.  **Tactic policy imitation**
    *   predict tactic template + (optional) lemma choice from `state`

Optional “delta-aware” auxiliary objective (very aligned with your thesis):

*   predict _what changes_ (e.g., predict embedding of next goal state from current state + chosen action)  
    This pushes embeddings to encode “actionable structure,” not just similarity.

Training can happen on rented GPU. Inference runs locally.

* * *

The one-line “ideal embedding spec”
-----------------------------------

If we had to lock the design:

> **Typed expression DAG encoder with hash-consing + cached bottom-up embeddings + goal-conditioned hypothesis attention + dual heads (retrieval/policy), trained on proof traces with contrastive lemma relevance + imitation.**

That’s the embedding core that makes delta-state worth it.

* * *

If you want, the very next thing I’d do is define the concrete data format for exported Lean expressions (JSON schema for node kinds, children, constant IDs, binder depth). Once that schema is fixed, everything above becomes straightforward to implement and iterate on in Cursor.

Before you run any code: prerequisites (one-time)
-------------------------------------------------

Cursor Prompt 3 — Parse Lean `TacticState.pp` into delta-friendly blocks + fast unit tests

Paste this into Cursor:

```text
Now implement a robust parser for LeanDojo TacticState.pp strings to produce "blocks" suitable for delta caching.

We are NOT doing Lean AST yet. This is the string/block MVP:
- Split a pp string into:
  - context lines (hypotheses etc.)
  - one or more goals (each preceded by the "⊢" turnstile)
- Produce normalized blocks:
  - each context line becomes one block: "CTX: <line>"
  - each goal target line becomes one block: "GOAL: <target>"
- Also create stable hashes for blocks (sha1 hex) for caching.

Create/modify EXACTLY these files:

A) alphaproofpp/lean/pp_parser.py
- Define dataclasses:
  - GoalPP(context_lines: list[str], target: str)
  - ParsedPP(goals: list[GoalPP])
- Implement functions:
  - normalize_pp(pp: str) -> str
    - normalize newlines to '\n'
    - strip trailing spaces on each line
    - strip leading/trailing whitespace overall
  - parse_tactic_state_pp(pp: str) -> ParsedPP
    - Must support:
      - single-goal format: "a : Nat\n⊢ a = a"
      - multi-goal: multiple "⊢" occurrences (treat each as new goal; context is the lines immediately preceding it since last goal end)
    - If "⊢" is absent, return ParsedPP(goals=[])
  - blocks_from_parsed(parsed: ParsedPP) -> list[str]
    - Output in deterministic order: for each goal, output CTX blocks then GOAL block
  - hash_block(block: str) -> str  (sha1 hex)
  - hash_blocks(blocks: list[str]) -> list[str]

B) tests/test_pp_parser.py
- Unit tests (fast, no LeanDojo needed) covering:
  1) single goal parsing
  2) multiple goal parsing
  3) normalization: trailing spaces removed
  4) blocks + hashes are deterministic

Constraints:
- Do not depend on LeanDojo types in this parser; operate purely on strings.
- Use stdlib only (hashlib, dataclasses, re).
```

Cursor Prompt 4 — OpenAI embedder with SQLite cache (critical for speed + cost)

Paste this into Cursor:

```text
Implement a production-quality OpenAI text embedding client with a local SQLite cache.

Requirements:
- Use the official OpenAI python client style:
  from openai import OpenAI
  client = OpenAI()
  client.embeddings.create(model=..., input=...)
- Model defaults to settings.openai_embed_model (default text-embedding-3-small).
- Optional dimensions: if settings.openai_embed_dimensions is set, pass it as `dimensions=...`.
- Cache embeddings in SQLite so repeated calls do not hit the API.

Create/modify EXACTLY these files:

A) alphaproofpp/embeddings/openai_embedder.py
- Create protocol/interface:
  - class TextEmbedder(Protocol):
      def embed_texts(self, texts: list[str]) -> "np.ndarray": ...
- Implement class OpenAIEmbedder(TextEmbedder):
  - __init__(self, model: str, api_key: str | None, cache_path: str, dimensions: int | None = None, batch_size: int = 64)
  - embed_texts(self, texts: list[str]) -> np.ndarray
    - returns float32 numpy array of shape (len(texts), dim)
    - must preserve input order
    - must deduplicate identical texts within a call (compute once, reuse)
- SQLite schema:
  - table embeddings(
      key TEXT PRIMARY KEY,
      model TEXT NOT NULL,
      dimensions INTEGER,
      text TEXT NOT NULL,
      vec BLOB NOT NULL
    )
- Cache key = sha256(model + "|" + str(dimensions or "") + "|" + text)
- Store vec as float32 bytes (np.ndarray.tobytes()).
- Load vec as np.frombuffer(..., dtype=np.float32).
- Add minimal retry logic (3 tries with exponential backoff) for transient API errors.

B) alphaproofpp/embeddings/__init__.py (empty)

  - calling embed_texts twice on same inputs
  - assert second call does not call the stub again (track call count)
- Keep test hermetic.
```

Batch 2 — Prompt 6 (Delta-cached embeddings)
============================================

Paste this into Cursor **exactly**:

```text
We already have:
- alphaproofpp/lean/pp_parser.py (parse_tactic_state_pp, blocks_from_parsed, hash_block, normalize_pp)
- alphaproofpp/embeddings/openai_embedder.py (TextEmbedder, OpenAIEmbedder w/ sqlite cache)
- alphaproofpp/lean/dojo_env.py (DojoEnv)
- settings/logging

Now implement a delta-cached proof-state embedder that only calls the underlying TextEmbedder for NEW blocks (hash-based), with in-memory cache, and expose a smoke CLI.

Create/modify EXACTLY these files:

A) alphaproofpp/embeddings/delta_state_embedder.py
- Implement:
  - @dataclass(frozen=True) class DeltaEmbedStats:
      num_blocks: int
      cache_hits: int
      cache_misses: int
      cache_size: int
  - @dataclass(frozen=True) class DeltaEmbeddedState:
      pp: str
      blocks: list[str]
      block_hashes: list[str]
      block_vecs: np.ndarray         # shape (num_blocks, dim), dtype float32
      state_vec: np.ndarray          # shape (dim,), dtype float32
- Implement class DeltaBlockStateEmbedder:
  - __init__(self, text_embedder: TextEmbedder)
    - maintain internal dict cache: Dict[str, np.ndarray] mapping block_hash -> vec (float32 1D array)
    - maintain internal _dim: int | None and _zero_vec: np.ndarray | None
  - clear_cache(self) -> None
  - embed_pp(self, pp: str) -> tuple[DeltaEmbeddedState, DeltaEmbedStats]
    Steps:
    1) Normalize pp using normalize_pp(pp).
    2) Parse with parse_tactic_state_pp, then blocks_from_parsed.
    3) Compute block_hashes via hash_block(block) for each block.
    4) Determine which hashes are missing from in-memory cache.
       - IMPORTANT: deduplicate missing blocks by hash while preserving first-seen order.
    5) If there are missing blocks:
       - call self._text_embedder.embed_texts(missing_texts) once
       - store each returned vector in cache by its hash
       - ensure stored vectors are np.float32 1D arrays
       - set self._dim if it is None from the embedding output dimension
    6) If blocks is empty:
       - if self._dim is None: compute it by calling embed_texts(["__ALPHAPROOFPP_DIM_PROBE__"]) and use that dim
         (Do NOT store the probe in cache; it is not a real block)
       - return state_vec = zeros(dim) and block_vecs = zeros((0, dim))
    7) Build block_vecs by looking up each hash in cache in the original order.
    8) Compute state_vec = mean pooling over block_vecs (axis=0).
  - Ensure returned arrays are contiguous float32.

- Add helper function mean_pool(block_vecs: np.ndarray) -> np.ndarray:
  - If block_vecs is empty, raise ValueError (handled by embed_pp before calling mean_pool).

B) alphaproofpp/cli/delta_state_embed_smoke.py
- CLI entrypoint: python -m alphaproofpp.cli.delta_state_embed_smoke
- Behavior:
  1) Load settings (get_settings()).
  2) Require OPENAI_API_KEY; if missing, print clear error and exit(2).
  3) Create DojoEnv for the same lean4-example hello_world theorem used earlier.
  4) Reset to get initial state; capture pp = state.pp.
  5) Create OpenAIEmbedder with:
     - model=settings.openai_embed_model
     - api_key=settings.openai_api_key
     - cache_path=os.path.join(settings.appcache_dir, "embeddings.sqlite3")
     - dimensions=settings.openai_embed_dimensions
  6) Create DeltaBlockStateEmbedder(text_embedder=openai_embedder).
  7) Call embed_pp(pp) twice.
     - Print for each call:
       - num_blocks, hits, misses, cache_size
       - the blocks list
       - state_vec shape and first 8 dims
  8) Create pp2 by mutating ONLY the goal line:
     - pp2 = pp.replace("a + c + b", "a + b + c")
     - (assume hello_world goal contains that substring)
  9) Call embed_pp(pp2) and print stats again.
     - Expected: only 1 miss (the GOAL block changed) and CTX block should be a hit.

C) alphaproofpp/cli/__init__.py
- Ensure this file exists (can be empty) if not already.

D) tests/test_delta_state_embedder_unit.py
- No network calls.
- Create a FakeTextEmbedder implementing TextEmbedder:
  - embed_texts(texts) returns deterministic float32 vectors of fixed dim=8
    Example: for each text, compute sha1(text) and map first 8 bytes to floats in a deterministic way.
  - Track how many times embed_texts was called and which texts were embedded.
- Tests:
  1) Given pp1 = "a b c : Nat\n⊢ a + b + c = a + c + b"
     - First embed_pp(pp1) => misses == 2, hits == 0, cache_size == 2
     - Second embed_pp(pp1) => misses == 0, hits == 2, cache_size == 2
     - Ensure FakeTextEmbedder.embed_texts called exactly once total for the two calls.
  2) pp2 = pp1.replace("a + c + b", "a + b + c")
     - embed_pp(pp2) => misses == 1, hits == 1, cache_size == 3
     - Ensure embed_texts called one additional time for exactly the new GOAL block text.
  3) Empty pp:
     - embed_pp("") returns num_blocks=0 and state_vec shape (8,) with all zeros.

Constraints:
- Keep everything typed.
- Use only stdlib + numpy.
- Be extremely defensive about shape/dtype correctness.
```

Batch 2 — Prompt 7 (Portfolio + best-first search to solve hello\_world)
========================================================================

Paste this into Cursor **only after Prompt 6 passes**:

```text
Now implement a minimal prover using:
- LeanDojo verifier loop (DojoEnv)
- A small portfolio of tactic strings
- Best-first search over proof states using a cheap heuristic derived from TacticState.pp parsing

We are NOT using LLM tactic generation yet. This is purely to build the end-to-end search/verifier infrastructure.

Create/modify EXACTLY these files:

A) alphaproofpp/prover/search.py
- Define:
  - @dataclass(frozen=True) class SearchConfig:
      max_expansions: int = 50
      max_depth: int = 5
  - @dataclass(frozen=True) class SearchResult:
      status: str                    # "solved" | "failed" | "timeout"
      tactics: list[str]
      expansions: int
      final_pp: str | None
- Implement:
  - def heuristic_score(pp: str, depth: int) -> tuple[int, int, int]:
      Use pp_parser.parse_tactic_state_pp to compute:
        num_goals = len(parsed.goals)
        target_chars = sum(len(g.target) for g in parsed.goals)
      Return (num_goals, target_chars, depth)
      Note: if num_goals == 0, treat as (9999, 999999, depth)
  - def best_first_solve(env: DojoEnv, start_state: object, tactics: list[str], config: SearchConfig) -> SearchResult:
      Behavior:
      1) Use env.pretty(state) to obtain pp. Only expand if the object is a TacticState.
      2) Use normalize_pp(pp) as the visited key. Maintain visited set[str].
      3) Priority queue items: (score_tuple, counter, state, pp, depth, tactic_trace)
      4) Pop best item; if depth > max_depth: continue.
      5) For each tactic in tactics (in given order):
         - Try env.run_tactic(state, tactic)
         - If ProofFinished: return status="solved" with trace + [tactic]
         - If TacticState: get new_pp; if normalize_pp(new_pp) not visited and new_pp != pp, push to queue
         - If LeanError or other: ignore
      6) Stop when expansions reaches max_expansions:
         return status="timeout" if queue not empty else "failed"
      7) final_pp should be the pp of the last popped node if available.

B) alphaproofpp/prover/portfolio.py
- Implement def default_tactic_portfolio() -> list[str]
- Include at minimum (in this exact order):
  1) "simp"
  2) "aesop"
  3) "simp [add_assoc, add_comm, add_left_comm]"
  4) "rw [add_assoc, add_comm b, ←add_assoc]"   # known to solve hello_world
  5) "ring"
  6) "omega"
- Keep it a plain list[str] for now.

C) alphaproofpp/prover/__init__.py
- Export SearchConfig, SearchResult, best_first_solve, default_tactic_portfolio.

D) alphaproofpp/cli/solve_hello_world.py
- CLI entrypoint: python -m alphaproofpp.cli.solve_hello_world
- Steps:
  1) load settings; warn if GITHUB token missing
  2) open DojoEnv for hello_world
  3) reset to get start_state
  4) call best_first_solve(env, start_state, default_tactic_portfolio(), SearchConfig(max_expansions=50,max_depth=3))
  5) print result: status, expansions, tactics trace
  6) if not solved: exit(1)
  7) if solved: print "SOLVED" and exit(0)

E) tests/test_search_unit.py
- No LeanDojo calls.
- Create a FakeEnv class with:
  - pretty(state)->str
  - run_tactic(state,tactic)->state_or_finished
- Define simple toy state transitions to test:
  - "A" --t1--> "B" --t2--> FINISHED
  - invalid tactics raise exception or return error marker
- Assert best_first_solve finds the solution trace ["t1","t2"] under small budgets.
- Assert visited prevents infinite loops.

Constraints:
- No network in tests.
- Keep types strict.
- Do not import any private LeanDojo internals.
```

Batch 3 Overview
================

### Deliverables by end of Batch 3

1.  **Premise DB builder**: Trace repo+deps via LeanDojo, extract “premise definitions” (full\_name + statement code), store as JSONL.
2.  **Premise embedding index**: Embed those premises using OpenAI embeddings (cached), store vectors to disk, load fast.
3.  **Retriever**: Given a proof state `pp`, retrieve top‑K premises by cosine similarity.
4.  **Retrieval‑augmented solver**: Use retrieved premises to generate tactics like:
    *   `simp [p1, p2, p3, ...]`
    *   `rw [p1]`, `rw [p1, p2]`
    *   `exact p1`, `apply p1`  
        …inside best‑first search.

We’ll validate everything on the same LeanDojo hello\_world theorem first (because it’s stable), then you can immediately point it at harder problems after.

```text
Now implement:
- a PremiseIndex stored on disk (metadata jsonl + vectors npy)
- a PremiseRetriever that uses our existing DeltaBlockStateEmbedder (or any state embedder) as the query embedder
- cosine similarity search implemented in pure numpy (no faiss yet)

Create/modify EXACTLY these files:

A) alphaproofpp/retrieval/index.py
- Define:
  - @dataclass(frozen=True) class PremiseIndexRecord:
      full_name: str
      code: str
      kind: str
      file_path: str
  - class PremiseIndex:
      - __init__(self, records: list[PremiseIndexRecord], vecs: np.ndarray)
        * vecs shape (N, D), float32
        * internally store normalized vectors self._vecs_unit
      - @property def dim(self) -> int
      - @property def size(self) -> int
      - def search(self, query_vec: np.ndarray, k: int) -> list[tuple[PremiseIndexRecord, float]]
        * normalize query_vec to unit
        * compute scores = self._vecs_unit @ query_unit (dot product)
        * return top-k records with scores sorted desc
  - def save_index(out_dir: str, records: list[PremiseIndexRecord], vecs: np.ndarray) -> None
    * out_dir contains:
      - premises.jsonl  (records)
      - vecs.npy        (float32 matrix)
  - def load_index(in_dir: str) -> PremiseIndex
    * read jsonl + npy and validate dimensions

B) alphaproofpp/retrieval/build.py
- Implement:
  - def format_premise_text(full_name: str, code: str, max_chars: int = 4000) -> str
    * Return a string used for embedding. Use:
      "NAME: <full_name>\nCODE:\n<code>"
    * Truncate to max_chars (hard truncate).
  - def build_premise_index_from_jsonl(premises_jsonl: str, out_dir: str, embedder: TextEmbedder, max_items: int | None = None) -> None
    Steps:
    1) load premises via load_premises_jsonl
    2) optionally slice to max_items
    3) build records list
    4) embed texts in batches using embedder.embed_texts
    5) save_index(out_dir, records, vecs)

    - must print progress via tqdm (already a dep)
    - must avoid recomputing embeddings thanks to OpenAIEmbedder sqlite cache

  - __init__(self, index: PremiseIndex, state_embedder: "DeltaBlockStateEmbedder" | "DeltaBlockStateEmbedder-compatible", premise_k: int = 32)
  - def retrieve_from_pp(self, pp: str, k: int | None = None) -> list[tuple[PremiseIndexRecord, float]]
    - embed pp using state_embedder.embed_pp(pp) and take embedded_state.state_vec as query
    - call index.search(query, k or self.premise_k)

D) alphaproofpp/retrieval/__init__.py
- Export PremiseIndex, PremiseRetriever, build_premise_index_from_jsonl, load_index.
```

Batch 3 — Prompt 10: Retrieval‑augmented tactic generation + search
===================================================================

Paste this into Cursor **exactly**:

```text
Now integrate retrieval into proving.

Implement:
- a function that turns retrieved premise names into a small set of tactic strings
- a retrieval-augmented best-first solver that tries:
  - static portfolio tactics
  - plus tactics synthesized from retrieved premises

Create/modify EXACTLY these files:

A) alphaproofpp/prover/retrieval_tactics.py
- Implement:
  - def synthesize_tactics_from_premises(premise_names: list[str]) -> list[str]
    Requirements:
    - premise_names are fully-qualified Lean names (strings)
    - generate a SMALL bounded set of tactics (avoid combinatorial explosion)
    - return in deterministic order, no duplicates

    Use this exact strategy:
    1) Let P = first 8 premise_names (if fewer, use all)
    2) Add:
       - f"simp [{', '.join(P[:3])}]" if len(P)>=3
       - f"simp [{', '.join(P[:8])}]" if len(P)>=8
       - f"rw [{P[0]}]" if len(P)>=1
       - f"rw [{P[0]}, {P[1]}]" if len(P)>=2
       - f"exact {P[0]}" if len(P)>=1
       - f"apply {P[0]}" if len(P)>=1
    3) Also add "simp" and "aesop" at the beginning regardless.

  - def merge_tactics(base: list[str], extra: list[str], max_total: int = 32) -> list[str]
    - keep order: all base first, then extra
    - deduplicate while preserving first occurrence
    - truncate to max_total

B) alphaproofpp/prover/retrieval_search.py
- Implement:
  - @dataclass(frozen=True) class RetrievalSearchConfig:
      max_expansions: int = 80
      max_depth: int = 6
      premise_k: int = 20
      max_tactics_per_node: int = 32
  - def best_first_solve_with_retrieval(
        env: DojoEnv,
        start_state: object,
        base_tactics: list[str],
        retriever: PremiseRetriever,
        config: RetrievalSearchConfig,
    ) -> SearchResult
    Behavior:
    - Similar to best_first_solve, but for each expanded node:
      1) retrieve top config.premise_k premises from pp
      2) synthesize extra tactics from their names
      3) try merged tactics (base + extra) limited to config.max_tactics_per_node
    - Keep visited by normalized pp as before
    - Return SearchResult as in prover/search.py

C) alphaproofpp/cli/solve_hello_world_retrieval.py
- CLI: python -m alphaproofpp.cli.solve_hello_world_retrieval
- Behavior:
  1) Load settings; require OPENAI_API_KEY else exit(2)
  2) Load index from <APPCACHE_DIR>/premises/index-lean4-example
  3) Construct OpenAIEmbedder + DeltaBlockStateEmbedder
  4) Construct PremiseRetriever(index, delta_embedder, premise_k=20)
  5) Open DojoEnv hello_world, reset
  6) Run best_first_solve_with_retrieval using:
     - base_tactics = default_tactic_portfolio()
     - config max_expansions=80, max_depth=5, premise_k=20, max_tactics_per_node=32
  7) Print:
     - status, expansions, tactic trace
     - if solved print SOLVED and exit(0), else exit(1)

D) tests/test_retrieval_tactics_unit.py
- Pure unit tests:
  - premise_names=["A","B","C","D","E","F","G","H","I"]
  - ensure deterministic output order
  - ensure no duplicates
  - ensure max_total limit works in merge_tactics

Constraints:
- No LeanDojo in tests.
- Keep code typed and defensive.
```

Batch 4 — Prompt 11: LLM tactic proposer (OpenAI Responses + schema + caching)
------------------------------------------------------------------------------

Paste this into Cursor **exactly**:

```text
Implement an OpenAI-based tactic proposer using the Responses API with Structured Outputs (JSON schema), plus a local SQLite cache.

We already have OpenAI dependency and embedding code; now we add generation.

Create/modify EXACTLY these files:

A) .env.example
- Add these lines (with defaults):
  OPENAI_CHAT_MODEL=gpt-4o-mini
  OPENAI_TACTIC_TEMPERATURE=0
  OPENAI_TACTIC_MAX_OUTPUT_TOKENS=256
  OPENAI_TACTIC_NUM_CANDIDATES=8
  OPENAI_TACTIC_CACHE_DB=tactic_cache.sqlite3

B) alphaproofpp/settings.py
- Extend Settings with:
  - openai_chat_model: str = "gpt-4o-mini"                (env OPENAI_CHAT_MODEL)
  - openai_tactic_temperature: float = 0.0                (env OPENAI_TACTIC_TEMPERATURE)
  - openai_tactic_max_output_tokens: int = 256            (env OPENAI_TACTIC_MAX_OUTPUT_TOKENS)
  - openai_tactic_num_candidates: int = 8                 (env OPENAI_TACTIC_NUM_CANDIDATES)
  - openai_tactic_cache_db: str = "tactic_cache.sqlite3"  (env OPENAI_TACTIC_CACHE_DB)
- Update tests in tests/test_settings.py accordingly.

C) alphaproofpp/llm/schema.py
- Define pydantic models:
  - class TacticSuggestions(BaseModel):
      tactics: list[str]
    With validators:
      - strip whitespace on each tactic
      - drop empty strings
      - deduplicate while preserving order
      - cap to max 16 tactics
- Export this class.

D) alphaproofpp/llm/openai_tactic_proposer.py
- Implement:
  - @dataclass(frozen=True) class ProposeResult:
      tactics: list[str]
      cache_hit: bool
      model: str

  - class OpenAITacticProposer:
      __init__(
        self,
        api_key: str | None,
        model: str,
        temperature: float,
        max_output_tokens: int,
        num_candidates: int,
        cache_path: str,
      )

      propose(self, pp: str, premise_names: list[str]) -> ProposeResult
        - Uses a SQLite cache keyed by sha256 of:
          model|temperature|max_output_tokens|num_candidates|pp|(",".join(premise_names[:64]))
        - Cache schema:
          table tactic_cache(
            key TEXT PRIMARY KEY,
            model TEXT NOT NULL,
            created_at REAL NOT NULL,
            pp TEXT NOT NULL,
            premises TEXT NOT NULL,
            tactics_json TEXT NOT NULL
          )
        - If cache hit: return cached tactics.
        - Else: call OpenAI Responses API with Structured Outputs:
          Use OpenAI() client from `openai` package.
          Use client.responses.parse(...) with TacticSuggestions as text_format.
          Set store=False.
          Provide `input` as a list of messages:
            - role: "system": very strict instruction
            - role: "user": includes pp + premise names
        - Return the parsed tactics list.

      _build_system_prompt() -> str
        Must instruct:
          - Output MUST be Lean 4 tactic lines (single-line strings)
          - No explanations, no markdown
          - Prefer short tactics that make progress
          - Allowed tactics include: simp, aesop, rw, exact, apply, ring, nlinarith, linarith, omega
          - Use premise names ONLY if they appear in the provided list

      _build_user_prompt(pp: str, premise_names: list[str]) -> str
        Must include:
          - The exact proof state pp
          - A section "Available premises:" listing up to first 32 premise names, one per line
          - A section "Task:" ask for up to num_candidates tactics

      Important robustness:
        - If OpenAI key missing: raise RuntimeError with clear message.
        - Retry transient errors up to 3 times with exponential backoff.
        - Always return at least ["simp", "aesop"] if model returns empty list.

E) alphaproofpp/llm/__init__.py
- Export OpenAITacticProposer, ProposeResult, TacticSuggestions.

F) alphaproofpp/cli/llm_tactic_smoke.py
- CLI entrypoint: python -m alphaproofpp.cli.llm_tactic_smoke
- Behavior:
  1) Load settings; require OPENAI_API_KEY else exit(2).
  2) Load premise index from <APPCACHE_DIR>/premises/index-lean4-example (must exist).
  3) Open DojoEnv for hello_world and reset; pp = state.pp.
  4) Retrieve top 20 premises using existing PremiseRetriever + DeltaBlockStateEmbedder + OpenAIEmbedder (as in Batch 3).
  5) Call OpenAITacticProposer.propose(pp, [prem.full_name for prem in top premises]).
  6) Print:
     - cache_hit, model
     - each tactic on its own line with numbering.

G) tests/test_openai_tactic_proposer_unit.py
- No network calls.
- Test caching + parsing by:
  - Create proposer with cache_path=temp sqlite.
  - Monkeypatch proposer._call_openai(...) method (you should implement it) to return a fixed list.
  - Call propose(...) twice with same inputs and assert:
      cache_hit False then True
      underlying call invoked only once
  - Also test that returned tactics are cleaned/deduped.

Constraints:
- Use OpenAI Responses API style: client = OpenAI(); client.responses.parse(...)
- Use pydantic for schema.
- Use stdlib sqlite3.
- Strong typing everywhere.
```

Batch 4 — Prompt 12: LLM + retrieval + search integration (verifier remains boss)
---------------------------------------------------------------------------------

Paste this into Cursor **exactly**:

```text
Integrate LLM tactic proposals into our retrieval-augmented best-first search.

Create/modify EXACTLY these files:

A) alphaproofpp/prover/llm_retrieval_search.py
- Define:
  - @dataclass(frozen=True) class LLMSearchConfig:
      max_expansions: int = 120
      max_depth: int = 6
      premise_k: int = 20
      max_tactics_per_node: int = 40
      max_llm_calls_total: int = 12
      llm_call_depth_limit: int = 2        # only call LLM for shallow nodes by default
      llm_first: bool = True               # include LLM tactics before other extra tactics

  - @dataclass(frozen=True) class LLMSearchResult:
      status: str                          # "solved" | "failed" | "timeout"
      tactics: list[str]
      expansions: int
      final_pp: str | None
      llm_calls: int
      llm_cache_hits: int

- Implement:
  def best_first_solve_with_retrieval_and_llm(
      env: DojoEnv,
      start_state: object,
      base_tactics: list[str],
      retriever: PremiseRetriever,
      proposer: OpenAITacticProposer,
      config: LLMSearchConfig,
  ) -> LLMSearchResult

Behavior:
- Use the same visited logic (normalize_pp) as previous search.
- For each expanded node:
  1) Retrieve top premise_k premises.
  2) Build synthesized tactics from those premises using existing synthesize_tactics_from_premises.
  3) If llm_calls < max_llm_calls_total AND depth <= llm_call_depth_limit:
       - call proposer.propose(pp, premise_names)
       - increment llm_calls, and llm_cache_hits if cache_hit
       - get llm_tactics list
     else llm_tactics = []
  4) Merge tactics deterministically with merge_tactics:
       - Start from base_tactics
       - Then add either llm_tactics then synthesized tactics (if llm_first True),
         else synthesized then llm.
       - Truncate to max_tactics_per_node.
  5) Try tactics in order with env.run_tactic:
       - ProofFinished => return solved with updated trace
       - TacticState => push to PQ if new pp and not visited
       - errors ignored

- Priority queue score should reuse heuristic_score(pp, depth) from prover/search.py.

B) alphaproofpp/cli/solve_hello_world_llm.py
- CLI: python -m alphaproofpp.cli.solve_hello_world_llm
- Purpose: demonstrate that LLM is actually used.
- Behavior:
  1) Require OPENAI_API_KEY.
  2) Load index from <APPCACHE_DIR>/premises/index-lean4-example
  3) Build OpenAIEmbedder + DeltaBlockStateEmbedder + PremiseRetriever
  4) Build OpenAITacticProposer from settings (cache at <APPCACHE_DIR>/<OPENAI_TACTIC_CACHE_DB>)
  5) Open DojoEnv hello_world
  6) Use a base_tactics list derived from default_tactic_portfolio() but REMOVE the exact known solver:
       remove "rw [add_assoc, add_comm b, ←add_assoc]" if present
  7) Run best_first_solve_with_retrieval_and_llm with:
       max_expansions=120, max_depth=5, premise_k=20, max_tactics_per_node=40,
       max_llm_calls_total=8, llm_call_depth_limit=1, llm_first=True
  8) Print:
       status, expansions, llm_calls, llm_cache_hits, tactics trace
     Exit 0 if solved else 1.

C) alphaproofpp/prover/__init__.py
- Export LLMSearchConfig, LLMSearchResult, best_first_solve_with_retrieval_and_llm.

D) tests/test_llm_retrieval_search_unit.py
- No network / no LeanDojo.
- Create FakeEnv, FakeRetriever, FakeProposer.
- Ensure solver calls proposer at depth 0 and uses returned tactics to solve.
- Ensure llm_calls respects max_llm_calls_total.

Constraints:
- Deterministic behavior in tests.
- Strong typing.
```

Batch 5 — Prompt 16: Memory-first solver (reduces LLM calls immediately)
========================================================================

Paste into Cursor:

```text
Integrate MemoryTacticProposer into search, ahead of LLM.

We will create a new solver that merges tactics in this priority order:
1) base portfolio tactics
2) memory tactics (kNN from trace memory)
3) retrieval-synthesized tactics
4) LLM tactics (optional, budgeted)

Create/modify EXACTLY these files:

A) alphaproofpp/prover/memory_llm_retrieval_search.py
- Define:
  - @dataclass(frozen=True) class MemoryLLMSearchConfig:
      max_expansions: int = 140
      max_depth: int = 7
      premise_k: int = 20
      max_tactics_per_node: int = 48
      max_llm_calls_total: int = 8
      llm_call_depth_limit: int = 1
      memory_k: int = 6
      memory_min_score: float = 0.0
      memory_first: bool = True          # if True, put memory tactics before retrieval/LLM extras

  - @dataclass(frozen=True) class MemoryLLMSearchResult:
      status: str
      tactics: list[str]
      expansions: int
      final_pp: str | None
      llm_calls: int
      llm_cache_hits: int
      memory_used: int                  # number of nodes where memory produced >=1 tactic

- Implement:
  def best_first_solve_with_memory_retrieval_and_llm(
      env: DojoEnv,
      start_state: object,
      base_tactics: list[str],
      premise_index: PremiseIndex,
      state_embedder: DeltaBlockStateEmbedder,
      memory_index: MemoryIndex,
      proposer: OpenAITacticProposer,
      config: MemoryLLMSearchConfig,
  ) -> MemoryLLMSearchResult

Algorithm:
- Same best-first structure and visited logic as earlier.
- For each node:
  1) Compute pp via env.pretty(state); normalize_pp(pp) is visited key.
  2) Embed pp ONCE using state_embedder.embed_pp(pp); take query_vec = embedded_state.state_vec
  3) Retrieve premises by premise_index.search(query_vec, config.premise_k)
     premise_names = [rec.full_name for rec,score in results]
  4) Memory tactics:
     memory_hits = memory_index.search(query_vec, config.memory_k)
     memory_tactics = [r.tactic for (r,score) in memory_hits if score >= config.memory_min_score]
     dedupe preserving order
     if memory_tactics non-empty: increment memory_used by 1
  5) Retrieval synthesized tactics from premise_names using synthesize_tactics_from_premises
  6) LLM tactics only if llm_calls < max_llm_calls_total AND depth <= llm_call_depth_limit:
     proposer.propose(pp, premise_names) -> tactics; update llm_calls and llm_cache_hits
  7) Merge tactics deterministically:
     - Start with base_tactics
     - Then add memory_tactics
     - Then add retrieval synthesized tactics
     - Then add LLM tactics
     - Use merge_tactics(base, extra, max_total=config.max_tactics_per_node)
  8) Try tactics in order; ProofFinished => solved.

- Priority score uses existing heuristic_score(pp, depth) from prover/search.py.

B) alphaproofpp/cli/solve_hello_world_memory_only.py
- CLI: python -m alphaproofpp.cli.solve_hello_world_memory_only
- Purpose: prove memory can solve without LLM and without omega in base tactics.
- Behavior:
  1) Require OPENAI_API_KEY else exit(2)
  2) Load premise index from <APPCACHE_DIR>/premises/index-lean4-example
  3) Load memory index from <APPCACHE_DIR>/memory/index-hello-world
     (if missing instruct to run build_memory_from_hello_world_trace)
  4) Build OpenAIEmbedder + DeltaBlockStateEmbedder
  5) Build OpenAITacticProposer BUT set config.max_llm_calls_total=0 so it is never called
  6) base_tactics = default_tactic_portfolio() with BOTH of these removed if present:
       - "rw [add_assoc, add_comm b, ←add_assoc]"
       - "omega"
  7) Run best_first_solve_with_memory_retrieval_and_llm(...)
     config: max_expansions=80, max_depth=4, max_llm_calls_total=0, llm_call_depth_limit=0,
             memory_k=5, memory_min_score=0.0
  8) Print status, expansions, memory_used, llm_calls, tactics trace
  9) Exit 0 if solved else 1

C) alphaproofpp/prover/__init__.py
- Export MemoryLLMSearchConfig, MemoryLLMSearchResult, best_first_solve_with_memory_retrieval_and_llm.

D) tests/test_memory_llm_retrieval_search_unit.py
- No network, no LeanDojo.
- Use FakeEnv + deterministic transitions such that:
  - base tactics do not solve
  - memory tactic solves
- Ensure memory_used increments and llm_calls stays 0 when max_llm_calls_total=0.

Constraints:
- Must embed pp once per node and reuse query_vec for memory + premise retrieval.
- Typed and defensive.
```

## Response:
Thought for 22m 57s

You’re absolutely good to go. ✅ And the numbers you’re seeing are the _right_ kind of progress:

*   You now have **a working proof OS**: verifier loop + search + retrieval + LLM proposer + trace logging + **memory replay**.
*   The “memory-only solved with 0 expansions + 0 LLM calls” result is _exactly_ what we mean by **compression-progress**: once a successful trajectory exists, we can reuse it cheaply.

How far are we from the product we originally aimed for?
--------------------------------------------------------

Right now we’ve built the **scaffolding that makes AlphaProof-style learning possible**. But we’re still _very far_ from “better than AlphaProof” in the sense people usually mean (IMO-level performance, broad generality, etc.).

AlphaProof’s edge comes from **massive-scale self-improvement loops** (AlphaZero-style RL in Lean, and for the hardest problems a kind of test-time RL generating/learning from huge numbers of variants).  
We don’t have that scale yet—we have the _hooks_.

### What we have now (big wins)

*   ✅ **Verifier-in-the-loop** (Lean is the judge)
*   ✅ **Search wrapper** (best-first)
*   ✅ **Retrieval conditioning**
*   ✅ **LLM as proposer (safe)**
*   ✅ **Trace logging**
*   ✅ **Memory index** (kNN reuse = immediate compression)

This is already “real” and non-trivial.

### What we’re missing for “AlphaProof competitor”

*   ❌ **Data scale** (you need thousands → millions of proof steps)
*   ❌ **A trained local policy/value model** (to stop paying LLM calls)
*   ❌ **AlphaZero-grade search** (MCTS + value guidance, not just best-first heuristics)
*   ❌ **True delta-state structural encoder** (AST/DAG caching, so compute scales with change)
*   ❌ **Curriculum + self-play / conjecture generation** (to keep frontier moving)

So: we’re at the end of the **bootstrap phase**. Next comes **compounding**.

“How many more batches until we beat AlphaProof?”
-------------------------------------------------

Two honest answers depending on what “beat” means:

*   If “beat” = **best laptop prover per dollar/compute**, with low LLM usage, strong on lots of mathlib-style tasks:  
    You’re about **~3–5 more batches** away from something that feels _shockingly strong_ for its size (because memory + distillation + better search + caching really compound).
*   If “beat AlphaProof” = **outperform DeepMind’s AlphaProof on hard benchmarks like IMO-style problems**:  
    That’s **not a fixed number of batches**. It becomes a research program + compute scaling problem, because AlphaProof-style systems rely on massive training/self-play/test-time compute.  
    We can still _aim_ there, but it won’t be “a few more batches and done.”

What we _can_ do (and it’s aligned with our whole conversation) is:

*   **compress** expensive search/LLM into cheap local behavior,
*   **scale task volume**,
*   **tighten the loop** (learn → solve more → learn more),
*   and then decide where to spend cloud compute strategically (teacher distillation), rather than brute forcing everything.

* * *

**turn traces into a cheap learned proposer**, so you’re not dependent on kNN + LLM.

Batch 6 — Prompt 17: Canonicalize tactics + build a supervised dataset from traces
----------------------------------------------------------------------------------

```text
Implement tactic canonicalization and build a dataset from JSONL traces.

We want to compress "what worked" into a small action space first.
Action space = tactic templates:
  simp, aesop, omega, ring, linarith, nlinarith, exact, apply, rw, simp_lemmas

Create/modify EXACTLY these files:

A) alphaproofpp/policy/tactic_canon.py
- Define:
  - class TacticTemplate(str, Enum):
      SIMP="simp"
      AESOP="aesop"
      OMEGA="omega"
      RING="ring"
      LINARITH="linarith"
      NLINARITH="nlinarith"
      EXACT="exact"
      APPLY="apply"
      RW="rw"
      SIMP_LEMMAS="simp_lemmas"

  - @dataclass(frozen=True) class CanonicalTactic:
      template: TacticTemplate
      args: list[str]          # lemma names if extractable, else empty
      original: str

- Implement:
  - def canonicalize_tactic(tactic: str) -> CanonicalTactic | None
    Rules (strict, deterministic):
    1) strip whitespace; collapse internal whitespace to single spaces.
    2) If tactic exactly matches one of:
       "simp","aesop","omega","ring","linarith","nlinarith" => corresponding template.
    3) If tactic startswith "exact " => EXACT; args=[first token after exact]
    4) If tactic startswith "apply " => APPLY; args=[first token after apply]
    5) If tactic startswith "rw [" and endswith "]" => RW
       - extract inside brackets, split by commas
       - for each item: strip spaces; remove leading "←" if present
       - take lemma_name = first whitespace-separated token (e.g., "add_comm b" -> "add_comm")
       - args = list of lemma_name tokens, keeping order, dropping empties
    6) If tactic startswith "simp [" and endswith "]" => SIMP_LEMMAS
       - parse args the same way as rw
    7) Otherwise return None.

B) alphaproofpp/policy/dataset.py
- Define:
  - @dataclass(frozen=True) class TemplateDataset:
      X: np.ndarray      # float32, shape (N,D)
      y: np.ndarray      # int64, shape (N,)
      label_to_id: dict[str, int]
      id_to_label: dict[int, str]
      meta: dict[str, object]

- Implement:
  - def build_template_dataset_from_traces(
        traces_jsonl: str,
        memory_index_dir: str,
        out_dir: str,
    ) -> None

  Behavior:
  - Load MemoryIndex from memory_index_dir (pairs.jsonl + vecs.npy). This provides (vec, tactic).
  - For each MemoryRecord:
      canonicalize tactic; if None, skip
      map template string to label id (create stable mapping sorted by label at end)
  - Build X (float32) from vecs, y (int64) from label ids, only for kept rows.
  - Save to out_dir:
      X.npy, y.npy, labels.json (label_to_id), meta.json
    meta includes:
      total_records, kept_records, skipped_records, dim

  Note:
  - traces_jsonl is only used for metadata provenance; do not embed again here.
  - Raise RuntimeError if kept_records == 0.

C) alphaproofpp/policy/__init__.py
```

Batch 6 — Prompt 18: Train a tiny local policy model (fast, CPU-friendly)

```text
Add a simple template policy model trained from the dataset, saved to disk, and loadable.

Use scikit-learn for speed.

Create/modify EXACTLY these files:
```
