```text
      model_path: str
      labels_path: str
      dim: int

  - def train_template_policy(dataset_dir: str, out_dir: str) -> TrainedTemplatePolicy
    Steps:
    1) Load X.npy, y.npy, labels.json from dataset_dir
    2) If y has <2 unique classes:
         - train a DummyClassifier(strategy="most_frequent")
       else:
         - train LogisticRegression(max_iter=500, n_jobs=1, multi_class="auto")
    3) Save model to out_dir/model.joblib
    4) Copy labels.json to out_dir/labels.json
    5) Write out_dir/meta.json with:
         num_samples, dim, num_classes, model_type
    6) Return TrainedTemplatePolicy paths

  - def load_template_policy(model_dir: str) -> tuple[object, dict[str,int], dict[int,str]]
    - Load joblib model
    - Load labels.json and build id_to_label
```

Batch 6 — Prompt 19: Use the trained policy as a proposer (reduces LLM calls)
-----------------------------------------------------------------------------

```text
Implement a TemplatePolicyProposer that:
- embeds pp once (we already do this in the memory+retrieval solver)
- uses the trained sklearn model to propose top templates
- converts templates into concrete tactics (using retrieved premise names when needed)

Create/modify EXACTLY these files:

A) alphaproofpp/policy/proposer.py
- Implement class TemplatePolicyProposer:
  - __init__(self, model_dir: str, top_k: int = 5)
    - load model + label maps via load_template_policy
  - def propose_templates(self, state_vec: np.ndarray) -> list[str]
    - If model has predict_proba: use it and return top_k labels by prob desc
    - Else: use predict and return [label]
  - def templates_to_tactics(self, templates: list[str], premise_names: list[str]) -> list[str]
    - Deterministic mapping:
      "simp" -> "simp"
      "aesop" -> "aesop"
      "omega" -> "omega"
      "ring" -> "ring"
      "linarith" -> "linarith"
      "nlinarith" -> "nlinarith"
      "rw" -> if premise_names non-empty: f"rw [{premise_names[0]}]" else skip
      "simp_lemmas" -> if len(premise_names)>=3: f"simp [{premise_names[0]}, {premise_names[1]}, {premise_names[2]}]" else skip
      "apply" -> if premise_names non-empty: f"apply {premise_names[0]}" else skip
      "exact" -> if premise_names non-empty: f"exact {premise_names[0]}" else skip
    - Deduplicate while preserving order.

B) alphaproofpp/prover/policy_memory_llm_search.py
- Add a new solver:
  - @dataclass(frozen=True) class PolicyMemoryLLMConfig:
      max_expansions: int = 140
      max_depth: int = 7
      premise_k: int = 20
      max_tactics_per_node: int = 48
      max_llm_calls_total: int = 6
      llm_call_depth_limit: int = 1
      memory_k: int = 6
      policy_top_k: int = 5
      policy_first: bool = True

  - @dataclass(frozen=True) class PolicyMemoryLLMResult:
      status: str
      tactics: list[str]
      expansions: int
      final_pp: str | None
      llm_calls: int
      llm_cache_hits: int
      memory_used: int
      policy_used: int

  - def best_first_solve_with_policy_memory_retrieval_and_llm(
        env: DojoEnv,
        start_state: object,
        base_tactics: list[str],
        premise_index: PremiseIndex,
        state_embedder: DeltaBlockStateEmbedder,
        memory_index: MemoryIndex,
        policy_proposer: TemplatePolicyProposer,
        llm_proposer: OpenAITacticProposer,
        config: PolicyMemoryLLMConfig,
    ) -> PolicyMemoryLLMResult

  Requirements:
  - Embed pp ONCE per node using state_embedder.embed_pp(pp); reuse query_vec.
  - Retrieve premise_names using premise_index.search(query_vec, premise_k)
  - Get policy templates from policy_proposer.propose_templates(query_vec) then policy_tactics = templates_to_tactics(...)
    If policy_tactics non-empty increment policy_used.
  - Get memory tactics from memory_index.search(query_vec, memory_k) as before; if non-empty increment memory_used.
  - Get retrieval-synth tactics via synthesize_tactics_from_premises(premise_names)
  - Get LLM tactics only if budget allows.
  - Merge order:
    base tactics
    then (if policy_first) policy tactics then memory tactics else memory then policy
    then retrieval synthesized
    then LLM
    truncate to max_tactics_per_node
  - Return metrics.

C) alphaproofpp/cli/eval_lean4_example_policy_first.py
- CLI: python -m alphaproofpp.cli.eval_lean4_example_policy_first
- Behavior:
  1) Require OPENAI_API_KEY
  2) Ensure tasks exist and premise index exists (same as eval_lean4_example_llm)
  3) Ensure model exists at <APPCACHE_DIR>/models/template-policy-lean4-example (else instruct to train)
  4) Ensure memory index exists at <APPCACHE_DIR>/memory/index-lean4-example (else instruct to build)
  5) Run evaluation on MAX_TASKS=20 using best_first_solve_with_policy_memory_retrieval_and_llm
  6) Print summary: solved/attempted and total LLM calls

D) alphaproofpp/prover/__init__.py
- Export PolicyMemoryLLMConfig, PolicyMemoryLLMResult, best_first_solve_with_policy_memory_retrieval_and_llm

E) tests/test_policy_proposer_unit.py
- No network.
- Create a fake model object with predict_proba returning fixed probs.
- Ensure propose_templates returns expected labels in order.
- Ensure templates_to_tactics maps correctly and dedupes.

Constraints:
- Strong typing; use Any for sklearn model.
- No network in unit tests.
```

Even on small tasks, you should see **LLM calls drop** as soon as the policy learns anything.

* * *

Why this batch matters for “beating AlphaProof”
-----------------------------------------------

This is the first real **compression step into weights**:

*   Memory (kNN) is “store everything.”
*   A trained policy is “compress what matters.”

To get anywhere near AlphaProof-level, we need **a lot more traces** than 2 theorems—this batch builds the mechanism so when you scale the task set, you actually _get compounding returns_ instead of just spending more on LLM calls.

* * *

If you run Batch 6 and paste:

*   the final `eval_lean4_example_policy_first` summary (solved/attempted + total LLM calls),  
    then Batch 7 will be: **scale tasks** (mathlib subset / miniF2F), and upgrade search (value-guided/MCTS-lite) so the learned policy actually matters on harder problems.

## Response:
Batch readiness check

Got it — I’ve updated the context with your MacBook specs for compute considerations.

Yep — **we’re good to go.** ✅  
And thanks for the Mac specs: an **M3 Max + 48GB** is _excellent_ for what we’re doing next because it lets you:

*   hold **much larger premise indices** in RAM
*   run **more expensive search** without paging
*   and later, plausibly run **local 7B-ish models** (MLX / llama.cpp Metal / Ollama) as proposers

That said, raw laptop compute still isn’t “DeepMind-scale RL training,” so the way we “beat AlphaProof” has to be **algorithmic leverage + compounding**, not brute force.

* * *

Where we are vs “better than AlphaProof”
----------------------------------------

Right now you’ve built the **core Proof OS**:

*   verifier loop ✅
*   retrieval ✅
*   LLM proposer ✅
*   logging ✅
*   memory replay ✅
*   distillation into a tiny local policy ✅

That’s the _hardest engineering foundation_.

What you **don’t** have yet (which AlphaProof has in spades) is:

1.  **Scale** (thousands → millions of proof steps)
2.  **A richer action space** (beyond templates + top‑1 lemma)
3.  **Value-guided search** (MCTS/value, not just heuristic best-first)
4.  **A true delta-state AST/DAG encoder** (so cost scales with change, not with pretty-printed text)

So: we’re past “toy,” and now we enter the phase where **data volume + better search** causes visible capability jumps.

### About “how many more batches”

*   If your target is: **a shockingly strong laptop prover on a meaningful mathlib subset**, we’re about **~3–5 batches** away (scale tasks → scale premises → better search → better policy → loop).
*   If your target is: **beat AlphaProof on IMO-style benchmarks**, there’s no fixed number of batches because it depends on compute and data scale; that becomes research + scaling. But the roadmap is still clear.

Next batch is the most important step for compounding: **scale beyond 2 theorems**.

* * *

Batch 7: Turn this into a “Project” pipeline + scale to mathlib subsets

Batch 7 gives you general-purpose tooling so you can point at _any repo/file_, generate tasks, build a **targeted premise index**, and run the full **bootstrap loop** (LLM → traces → memory → policy → eval).

We’ll do this in 3 prompts.

```text
    * Uses subprocess.run(["git","ls-remote",repo_url,ref], capture_output=True, text=True, check=True)
    * Parse first token of stdout (split whitespace), validate it's 40 hex chars.
```

### Build a _targeted_ premise DB + index from selected file prefixes

This is the key to scaling: you cannot embed all of mathlib at once; you build an index over a _slice_ that matches your task set.

Paste into Cursor:

```text
Implement targeted premise extraction by file prefix, and a generic premise-index builder CLI.

Create/modify EXACTLY these files:

A) alphaproofpp/premises/select.py

  - def select_traced_files_by_prefix(traced_repo: "TracedRepo", include_prefixes: list[str], max_files: int | None = None) -> list["TracedFile"]
    Rules:
      * traced_repo.traced_files contains TracedFile objects with .path
      * include_prefixes match on str(tf.path). Replace backslashes with "/" before matching.
      * deterministic order: sort by str(tf.path)
      * include if any prefix matches at start
      * if max_files set, truncate

B) alphaproofpp/premises/extract.py
- Add new function:
  - def extract_premises_from_traced_files(traced_files: list["TracedFile"], max_premises: int | None = None) -> list[Premise]
    * Iterate traced_files in order
    * For each tf, call tf.get_premise_definitions()
    * Build Premise objects
    * Deduplicate by full_name (keep first)
    * Stop if max_premises reached

C) alphaproofpp/cli/build_premise_db_from_repo.py
```

Transition cache + richer action generation with priors (deterministic)
-----------------------------------------------------------------------

Paste into Cursor **exactly**:

```text
Implement:
1) An in-memory transition cache for env.run_tactic keyed by (normalized_pp, tactic).
2) A richer action generator that produces tactic candidates WITH priors (float in [0,1]) from:
   - base portfolio tactics
   - memory hits (kNN)
   - template policy probs
   - retrieval results (premise names + similarity scores)
   - optional LLM proposals (tactic strings)

All must be deterministic, deduped, and bounded (no combinatorial explosion).

Create/modify EXACTLY these files:

A) alphaproofpp/prover/transitions.py
- Implement:
  - @dataclass(frozen=True) class TransitionStats:
      cache_hits: int
      cache_misses: int
      attempted: int

  - @dataclass(frozen=True) class TransitionOutcome:
      kind: str            # "state" | "finished" | "error"
      result: object       # LeanDojo object (TacticState/ProofFinished/LeanError/etc.)
      next_pp: str | None  # pp for next state when kind=="state"

  - class TransitionCache:
      def __init__(self) -> None:
         self._cache: dict[tuple[str, str], TransitionOutcome] = {}
         self._hits=0; self._misses=0; self._attempted=0

      def stats(self) -> TransitionStats
      def clear(self) -> None

      def run(
          self,
          env: "DojoEnv",
          state: object,
          state_pp_norm: str,
          tactic: str,
      ) -> TransitionOutcome

    Behavior:
    - key=(state_pp_norm, tactic)
    - if cached: hits++, attempted++, return cached
    - else: attempted++, call env.run_tactic(state, tactic)
        * If ProofFinished: store kind="finished", next_pp=None
        * If TacticState: compute next_pp = env.pretty(next_state); store kind="state", next_pp=normalize_pp(next_pp)
        * Else: kind="error"
      cache it; misses++
    - Never raise on tactic failure; only raise on unexpected env exceptions, wrap in RuntimeError with context.

B) alphaproofpp/prover/actions.py
- Implement:
  - @dataclass(frozen=True) class Action:
      tactic: str
      prior: float
      source: str  # "base"|"memory"|"policy"|"retrieval"|"llm"
  - def clamp01(x: float) -> float
  - def dedupe_actions(actions: list[Action], max_total: int) -> list[Action]
    * Deterministic:
      - preserve first occurrence order by tactic string
      - if same tactic appears again, keep the higher prior but DO NOT change its position
    * Truncate to max_total
  - def score_to_prior(score: float) -> float
    * Convert cosine score in [-1,1] to [0,1] via (score+1)/2 then clamp.

C) alphaproofpp/prover/action_gen.py
- Implement:
  - @dataclass(frozen=True) class ActionGenConfig:
      max_actions: int = 64
      premise_k: int = 20
      apply_k: int = 8         # how many top premises to use for apply/exact
      rw_single_k: int = 4
      rw_pair_k: int = 3
      simp_k: int = 8
      include_llm: bool = True
      llm_depth_limit: int = 1

  - def actions_from_base(base_tactics: list[str]) -> list[Action]
    * prior = 0.20 for "simp", 0.18 for "aesop", else 0.10 for others, source="base"

  - def actions_from_memory(memory_hits: list[tuple[str,float]]) -> list[Action]
    * memory_hits input is list[(tactic, score)] score assumed cosine-like; convert to prior via score_to_prior
    * source="memory"; add +0.20 bonus then clamp

  - def actions_from_policy_templates(
        templates: list[tuple[str,float]],
        premise_names: list[str],
    ) -> list[Action]
    * templates input list[(template_label, prob)] where prob in [0,1]
    * Map templates to tactics deterministically:
      "simp","aesop","omega","ring","linarith","nlinarith" -> same string
      "apply","exact" -> for i < min(3,len(premise_names)): "apply <pi>" etc (generate up to 3 each)
      "rw" -> if premise_names: "rw [<p0>]" and if >=2 "rw [<p0>, <p1>]"
      "simp_lemmas" -> if >=3 "simp [p0, p1, p2]" and if >=8 "simp [p0..p7]"
    * prior = prob, source="policy"

  - def actions_from_retrieval(premises: list[tuple[str,float]], cfg: ActionGenConfig) -> list[Action]
    * premises input list[(premise_full_name, cosine_score)]
    * Use top cfg.apply_k for:
        "exact <name>" and "apply <name>" with priors = score_to_prior(score)
    * Use top cfg.rw_single_k for "rw [name]" priors = score_to_prior(score)
    * For rw pairs:
        take first cfg.rw_pair_k names; generate ordered pairs (p0,p1), (p0,p2), (p1,p2) etc up to 3 pairs total
        tactic = f"rw [{a}, {b}]"
        prior = average of the two priors
    * simp lemmas:
        if >=3: "simp [p0,p1,p2]" prior = avg top3 prior
        if >=cfg.simp_k: "simp [p0..p(simp_k-1)]" prior = avg of those priors
    * source="retrieval"

  - def actions_from_llm(tactics: list[str]) -> list[Action]
    * prior = 0.25 for each, source="llm"

  - def build_action_list(
        base_tactics: list[str],
        premise_results: list[tuple[str,float]],
        memory_hits: list[tuple[str,float]],
        policy_templates: list[tuple[str,float]],
        llm_tactics: list[str],
        cfg: ActionGenConfig,
    ) -> list[Action]
    * Combine in this order:
      base -> policy -> memory -> retrieval -> llm
    * Deduplicate via dedupe_actions(max_total=cfg.max_actions)
```

MCTS-lite (PUCT) solver using transition cache + action priors
--------------------------------------------------------------

Paste into Cursor:

```text
Implement an MCTS-lite solver (PUCT) that:
- expands nodes by generating actions via build_action_list (from action_gen)
- uses TransitionCache to avoid repeated Lean calls
- uses heuristic leaf evaluation when not solved
- supports budgeted LLM calls only at shallow depth
- returns the first solved trace found

Create/modify EXACTLY these files:

A) alphaproofpp/prover/mcts.py
- Define:
  - @dataclass(frozen=True) class MCTSConfig:
      max_simulations: int = 80
      max_depth: int = 6
      c_puct: float = 1.2
      action_cfg: ActionGenConfig = ActionGenConfig()
      premise_k: int = 20
      memory_k: int = 6
      policy_top_k: int = 5
      llm_max_calls_total: int = 6
      llm_depth_limit: int = 1

  - @dataclass(frozen=True) class MCTSResult:
      status: str                 # "solved" | "failed" | "timeout"
      tactics: list[str]
      simulations: int
      transitions: TransitionStats
      llm_calls: int
      llm_cache_hits: int

- Internal classes (simple dataclasses):
  - class Edge:
      action: Action
      n: int
      w: float
      q: float
      child_pp: str | None

  - class Node:
      pp_norm: str
      state: object
      depth: int
      n: int
      edges: list[Edge] | None   # None until expanded

- Implement:
  - def heuristic_value(pp_norm: str) -> float
    * Use parse_tactic_state_pp(pp_norm) to compute num_goals and total target_chars.
    * Return value in (0,1]:
      v = exp(-(num_goals-1)*1.0) * exp(-(target_chars/80.0))
      Clamp to [0.0, 1.0].
      If num_goals==0: return 0.0

  - def select_edge_puct(node: Node, c_puct: float) -> int
    * Choose edge index maximizing:
        edge.q + c_puct * edge.action.prior * sqrt(node.n + 1) / (1 + edge.n)
    * Deterministic tie-breaker: smallest index.

  - def mcts_solve(
      env: DojoEnv,
      start_state: object,
      base_tactics: list[str],
      premise_index: PremiseIndex,
      state_embedder: DeltaBlockStateEmbedder,
      memory_index: MemoryIndex,
      policy_proposer: TemplatePolicyProposer,
      llm_proposer: OpenAITacticProposer,
      config: MCTSConfig,
  ) -> MCTSResult

Algorithm:
- Maintain:
  nodes: dict[str, Node]  # keyed by pp_norm
  transition_cache = TransitionCache()
  llm_calls=0, llm_cache_hits=0
- Initialize root:
  pp = env.pretty(start_state); pp_norm = normalize_pp(pp)
  nodes[pp_norm] = Node(pp_norm, start_state, depth=0, n=0, edges=None)
- For sim in range(max_simulations):
  - node = root; path: list[tuple[Node,int]] = []; tactics_path: list[str]=[]
  - for depth in range(max_depth):
      * If node.edges is None:
          - Build query_vec ONCE: embedded_state = state_embedder.embed_pp(pp_norm)[0].state_vec
            (NOTE: DeltaBlockStateEmbedder returns (DeltaEmbeddedState, stats). Use embedded_state.state_vec.)
          - premise_results = premise_index.search(query_vec, config.premise_k)
            -> list[(record, score)] ; convert to [(record.full_name, score)]
          - memory_hits = memory_index.search(query_vec, config.memory_k)
            -> list[(MemoryRecord, score)] ; convert to [(record.tactic, score)]
          - policy templates:
            - Use policy_proposer.propose_templates(query_vec) if it returns labels only.
              We need probs too: modify TemplatePolicyProposer (if needed) to expose template probs.
              If it cannot, treat returned templates as prob=0.20 each.
          - LLM tactics:
            - only if llm_calls < llm_max_calls_total AND node.depth <= llm_depth_limit:
                call llm_proposer.propose(pp_norm, premise_names)
                llm_calls++, llm_cache_hits += int(cache_hit)
                llm_tactics = result.tactics
              else llm_tactics=[]
          - Build actions list via build_action_list(...)
          - Set node.edges = [Edge(action=a, n=0, w=0.0, q=0.0, child_pp=None) for a in actions]
      * Select edge idx = select_edge_puct(node, c_puct)
      * edge = node.edges[idx]
      * Apply transition using transition_cache.run(env, node.state, node.pp_norm, edge.action.tactic)
        - If finished: return solved with tactics_path+[tactic]
        - If error: treat as value=0.0 and break rollout loop
        - If state:
            next_pp_norm = outcome.next_pp
            set edge.child_pp=next_pp_norm
            if next_pp_norm not in nodes:
                nodes[next_pp_norm] = Node(next_pp_norm, outcome.result, depth=node.depth+1, n=0, edges=None)
            append (node, idx) to path; append tactic to tactics_path; node = child; continue
  - Leaf evaluation:
      if terminated by error: leaf_value=0.0
      else: leaf_value = heuristic_value(node.pp_norm)
  - Backprop:
      for (n, idx) in path:
         n.n += 1
         e = n.edges[idx]
         e.n += 1
         e.w += leaf_value
         e.q = e.w / e.n

- If no solve after max_simulations: return status="timeout" if any sims run else "failed"
- Include transition_cache.stats() in result.

IMPORTANT MODIFICATION:
TemplatePolicyProposer currently returns templates only.
Update it to expose probabilities if model supports predict_proba:
- Add method propose_templates_with_probs(state_vec)->list[(label,prob)].
- If no probas, return [(pred_label,1.0)].

B) alphaproofpp/policy/proposer.py
- Add:
  - def propose_templates_with_probs(self, state_vec: np.ndarray) -> list[tuple[str,float]]
    * If model has predict_proba: return top_k (label, prob)
    * Else: return [(label, 1.0)] for predicted label
- Keep existing propose_templates as wrapper around this.

C) alphaproofpp/prover/__init__.py
- Export MCTSConfig, MCTSResult, mcts_solve.

D) tests/test_mcts_unit.py
- No LeanDojo.
- Create FakeEnv with deterministic transitions:
  - State "S0" + tactic "a" -> "S1"
  - "S1" + tactic "b" -> FINISHED
  - other tactics -> error
- Create stub premise_index/memory_index/policy_proposer/llm_proposer that produce actions including "a" then "b"
- Ensure mcts_solve returns solved ["a","b"] within small simulation budget.

Constraints:
- No network in tests.
- Keep imports light.
- Deterministic behavior.
```

## Response:
Thought for 20m 24s

Yep — you’re solid. ✅  
The screenshot tells me Batch 8 is doing what it should:

*   **MCTS works end‑to‑end** (PUCT selection → cached transitions → solved trace).
*   `solve_hello_world_mcts` solved with **1 simulation / 1 Lean transition** because the system immediately found a one-shot tactic (`omega`). That’s _expected_ given (a) memory/policy/LLM can propose it and (b) the problem is tiny.
*   `eval_tasks_mcts` solved **2/2 with 2 LLM calls**. Again, tasks are too small to show “search intelligence”; they mainly show the _plumbing_ works.

* * *

Where are we vs AlphaProof?
---------------------------

### Per‑unit‑compute (efficiency)

Right now, what you’ve demonstrated is **the compression mechanism works**:

*   If the system has seen a successful trace, it can “compile” that into memory/policy and solve with **almost no compute** (1 verifier call).
*   That’s a real ingredient of “intelligence = compression‑progress.”

But: **this isn’t yet an advantage over AlphaProof**, because AlphaProof would also trivially solve these toy theorems with low compute. What we’ve proven is that your Proof‑OS can _become efficient_ once it learns. That’s the right direction — it’s just not measurable yet on meaningful benchmarks.

### Absolute performance

In absolute terms, we’re **still far** from AlphaProof. The reason is simple:

*   We’re not yet running on the kind of datasets where AlphaProof shines (large Lean corpora, miniF2F/competition-style formalizations, deeper multi-step proofs).
*   Our current “state representation” is still **pretty-printed strings**, not the true **delta AST/DAG encoder** we originally envisioned.
*   Our current policy is a **template classifier**; AlphaProof-level systems use far richer action spaces + value-guided search + huge training loops.

So: **engineering foundation is excellent**; **capability is not yet in the same league**, because we haven’t scaled task volume or moved to structural deltas.

* * *

The next batch (Batch 9) is the right next move
===============================================

Batch 9’s purpose:  
**make the system cheaper to run and more measurable**, so scaling becomes realistic on your M3 Max.

We’re going to add:

1.  **Local tactic proposer (Ollama backend)** so you can spend search budget locally (no network)
2.  **A proposer “router”** so everything can switch OpenAI ↔ Ollama
3.  **Compute accounting + tactic bans** so we can measure and force harder behavior (stop `omega` from masking progress)

This is aligned with your thesis: _reduce compute per solved theorem_ by compressing into local models + caching, and push the system into regimes where it must do multi-step reasoning.

```text
      resp["message"]["content"] as text

  - Usage:

      else usage=None

Constraints:
```

## Response:
Thought for 31m 15s

Yep — we’re good to go. ✅ And your screenshot is _extremely_ informative.

### What your “omega-banned” result actually says

*   The system **did explore**: 80 sims, **83 verifier transitions**, and a **~60% transition cache hit rate** (50 hits / 83 attempted). That’s the _right_ signature for “efficiency via reuse.”
*   It **timed out** because once you remove the “big hammer” (`omega`) the remaining action space + priors weren’t strong enough to reliably land the right multi-step transform inside the budget.

That’s not a failure; it’s the first time you’re seeing the real game: **search + action space quality** matters more than “having a model.”

This also ties directly to the span/work framing you’ve noted before (algorithmic span, critical path): you’re now measuring something real — verifier calls and cache reuse are your “work,” proof depth is your “span.”

* * *

Batch 10 Plan (your answer is correct)
======================================

We target:

*   **file**: `Mathlib/Data/Nat/Basic.lean`
*   **premise prefix**: `Mathlib/Data/Nat/`

This is the right slice because it’s:

*   meaningful (not toy),
*   still small enough to iterate quickly,
*   rich in reusable algebraic patterns (perfect for “compression-progress”).

* * *

Are we starting the “revolutionary delta AST/DAG encoder” soon?
===============================================================

Yes — but **we need one thing first**: a **non-trivial dataset + stable eval loop** (which Batch 10 provides). Otherwise we can’t tell if the encoder is actually helping.

Here’s the roadmap (and why it’s the right order):

Stage A (now): scale + measure
------------------------------

*   Build a mathlib slice
*   Generate **hundreds/thousands of trace steps**
*   Measure:
    *   solved / attempted
    *   verifier\_attempted (Lean transitions)
    *   cache hit rate
    *   LLM tokens (or local proposer tokens)

Stage B (next 1–2 batches): “delta” without requiring Lean AST
--------------------------------------------------------------

We implement a **delta token-multiset encoder** (cheap, incremental) to prove the delta idea end-to-end:

*   maintain token counts of the pp
*   update counts by diff
*   embed via feature hashing / small learned projection  
    This is “delta-state” with real performance wins and no Lean internals required.

Stage C (later, the real thing): True Expr DAG encoder
------------------------------------------------------

That’s the “revolutionary” piece:

*   extract Lean goal expressions as **structured DAGs**
*   hash-cons nodes
*   message passing / bottom-up embeddings cached by node-hash
*   state embedding updates become proportional to **changed subgraph**, not total state size

This is the step that can genuinely change your “span” constant factors (CPIB-style): it cuts the critical path cost of _each search step_.

So no, I haven’t forgotten it — we’re setting it up so when we do it, we can **prove it matters**.

* * *

Thought experiments: “Large math models” + a natively mathematical model
========================================================================

You’re basically describing two different things that often get mixed up:

1) “Large math models” (informal math)
--------------------------------------

These are models trained to solve math **in natural language / LaTeX**. Examples:

*   **Minerva**: a large LM trained further on technical content for quantitative reasoning. [arXiv+1](https://arxiv.org/abs/2206.14858)
*   **Llemma**: an open LM for math (continued pretraining on math-heavy corpora) that can also do tool use and some formal theorem proving without fine-tuning. [OpenReview](https://openreview.net/forum?id=4WnqRR915j)

These are great for _ideas_, but they don’t have an ungameable judge unless you hook them to tools.

2) “Formal math models” (Lean-native)
-------------------------------------

These operate _inside_ a verifier loop and output Lean tactics/proofs.

*   DeepMind’s **AlphaProof** explicitly trains in **Lean** and couples a pretrained LM with **AlphaZero-style RL**. [Google DeepMind+1](https://deepmind.google/blog/ai-solves-imo-problems-at-silver-medal-level/)
*   Open work like **DeepSeek‑Prover‑V2** is explicitly Lean 4 formal theorem proving + RL for subgoal decomposition (huge model, but the method is key). [arXiv+1](https://arxiv.org/abs/2504.21801)

This category is closer to what you want.

What a “native mathematical model” really is
--------------------------------------------

It’s not “English → math.” It’s:

*   **typed structure in, typed actions out**
*   trained primarily by **verifier feedback**
*   with representations that match the underlying object: **AST/DAG of expressions**

That’s why your instincts about “language is not the right substrate” are correct.

But the punchline is important:

### You don’t necessarily need a non-transformer architecture

Most of the wins so far (including AlphaProof-style systems and many formal provers) come from:

*   better **training signal** (verifier)
*   better **search**
*   better **representation** (structured inputs)
*   better **curriculum/self-play loops**

Transformers can still be the policy/value function; the “revolutionary” part is how you **feed** them and how you **close the loop**.

### How we beat AlphaProof “per unit compute” from a laptop

Not by out-scaling it. By out‑_amortizing_ it:

*   caching (transitions, embeddings)
*   distilling traces into cheap policies
*   macro learning (compile frequent tactic sequences)
*   delta encoders that make each step cheaper

This is exactly the “compression-progress” thesis operationalized.

* * *

Where we are vs AlphaProof (honest positioning)
===============================================

Absolute terms
--------------

We are **far** from AlphaProof’s headline capability (competition-grade problems). AlphaProof’s key advantage is massive-scale self-improvement (LM + AlphaZero RL in Lean). [Google DeepMind](https://deepmind.google/blog/ai-solves-imo-problems-at-silver-medal-level/)

Right now you’ve proven the system architecture; you haven’t scaled the data/search enough for absolute comparison.

Per-unit-compute terms
----------------------

You’re now building the ingredients that _can_ win on efficiency:

*   transition cache hit rates
*   local policy/memory distillation
*   MCTS allocation rather than brute-force enumeration

But we need Batch 10 (mathlib slice) to make this measurable.

* * *

Batch 10 (next) — 3 prompts
===========================

Batch 10 has one mission: **scale to Nat/Basic and make everything fast enough to iterate**.

Prompt 29 — Scale indexes: memmap + chunked cosine top‑k
--------------------------------------------------------

This makes mathlib-size indices feasible on your M3 Max.

```text
Implement chunked cosine top-k search and memory-mapped loading for BOTH:
- PremiseIndex.search
- MemoryIndex.search

Goals:
- avoid allocating full score vectors for very large N
- support vecs stored as npy memmap
- deterministic results

Modify EXACTLY these files:

A) alphaproofpp/premises/index.py
- In load_index(...):
  - load vecs via np.load(vecs_path, mmap_mode="r")
  - ensure dtype float32 (cast view if needed)
- In PremiseIndex:
  - add init param chunk_size: int | None = None
  - implement search(query_vec, k, chunk_size: int | None = None)
    Behavior:
    - choose effective_chunk = chunk_size or self.chunk_size
    - if effective_chunk is None or N <= effective_chunk: keep existing fast path
    - else: do chunked top-k:
        for offset in range(0,N,effective_chunk):
          chunk = vecs_unit[offset:offset+effective_chunk]
          scores = chunk @ q_unit
          take top k from scores via argpartition
          merge into global best (keep arrays best_scores, best_idx)
        final sort by score desc then index asc
    - return list[(PremiseRecord, score)]

B) alphaproofpp/memory/index.py
- In load_memory_index(...):
  - load vecs via np.load(..., mmap_mode="r")
- In MemoryIndex:
  - add chunk_size: int | None = None
  - implement chunked search(query_vec, k) with same merge logic
  - keep unit-normalization invariant

C) tests/test_chunked_search_unit.py
- No network.
- Build a synthetic vec matrix (e.g., 1000x32) and a query.
- Compare:
  - unchunked search results == chunked search results (same top-k ids and scores close)
  - test for both PremiseIndex and MemoryIndex (use small fake record lists)

Constraints:
- typed and deterministic
- use numpy only
```

"""""Thanks — storage isn’t the blocker anymore. The blocker is **LeanDojo’s REPL handshake in the traced mathlib repo**. Below is a full, exhaustive report for you and GPT‑Pro.

## What’s happening (high‑level)
We can build the tasks/premises/index fine, but **Dojo fails to start for most theorems** because Lean’s REPL (Lean4Repl.lean) crashes on startup with:

[fatal] failed to parse JSON offset 0: unexpected end of input

That crash happens *inside* the Lean REPL loop and causes Lean to exit (exit code 1), so DojoInit fails and MCTS never runs.

I tried patching Lean4Repl to skip empty lines, but that **introduced a new failure**: the REPL never prints the initial REPL> {...} line anymore, so Dojo just times out.

So we are now stuck between:
- **Old behavior**: REPL prints initial JSON but crashes because it parses an empty line.
- **Patched behavior**: REPL no longer crashes, but never prints output / hangs.

---

## Evidence & outputs (key logs)

### 1) Original REPL crash (before patch)

### 2) Dojo error in MCTS eval (after fixing memory flag)

## Why this is not storage related
All failures are REPL/Dojo init errors or Lean crash errors. Storage is now fine; the errors occur immediately at REPL startup.

---

## Likely root cause (hypothesis)
Lean4Repl reads a line from stdin immediately, but gets an **empty line** (or EOF), and throws the fatal JSON parse error. That error **kills the Lean process**.

If we skip empty lines (patch), we avoid the parse error, but Lean now **never emits the initial JSON prompt**, so Dojo waits forever.

We need to fix the REPL’s startup handshake so it:
1. Prints the initial JSON response.
2. Ignores empty input *without blocking forever or suppressing output*.

---

## Recommendations / next debugging steps
These are the likely directions GPT‑Pro can help decide:

1. **Revert Lean4Repl patch** to restore “REPL prints output but crashes,” then design a correct fix.
2. Modify Lean4Repl loop to:
   - treat empty line as continue **but only after** the initial response is printed;
   - or treat empty line as break if EOF (avoid infinite loop).
3. Modify Dojo to send a dummy JSON command immediately *before* waiting for the first REPL output (so the first getLine isn’t empty).
4. Change how Lean is launched (avoid stdin EOF): perhaps pass a PTY that doesn’t deliver an empty line on startup.

## Response:
Thought for 33m 11s

Yep — **you’re not blocked by storage or by our AlphaProof++ architecture right now**. You’re blocked by a very specific (and _real_) plumbing bug:

*   **LeanDojo’s `Lean4Repl.lean` is calling `Json.parse` on an empty string** (coming from `stdin.getLine` returning `""`) → Lean throws a _fatal_ error → process exits → Dojo fails to start.
*   This matches an **open LeanDojo upstream issue**: “`lean_dojo_repl` not working” (#250). [GitHub](https://github.com/lean-dojo/LeanDojo/issues/250)
*   And your observation that “`getLine` returns empty” is consistent with Lean’s own stream semantics: if `getLine` returns an empty string, it indicates an **EOF marker**, _but the stream may still later return more data_ — meaning `""` must not be treated as a hard failure. [cs.rochester.edu](https://www.cs.rochester.edu/~yzhu104/lean-gccjit/Init/System/IO.html)

Why your “skip empty lines” patch caused the _new_ hang
-------------------------------------------------------

Your patch idea was correct in spirit, but the behavior you saw (“no initial `REPL>` line, Dojo times out”) almost always happens when the patch accidentally does one of these:

1.  **Moves the first `stdin.getLine` before the initial `REPL>` print**, so the program blocks waiting for input and never prints the initial handshake.
2.  **Turns the loop into a tight spin on empty reads** (especially if Lean keeps returning EOF markers), so it never reaches the code path that prints / flushes the initial message in a predictable way.

We want:

*   **Always print the initial `REPL>` JSON immediately**
*   Then loop forever reading commands
*   If a read yields `""` or whitespace → **sleep a few ms and retry** (don’t parse, don’t crash)

That’s the “correct fix”.

* * *

Hotfix Batch 10 (Cursor prompts)
================================

This batch is _purely_ about restoring a stable LeanDojo REPL so we can proceed to actual mathlib scaling + delta encoders + learning loops.

Prompt 10.1 — Implement the correct REPL fix (Lean side)
--------------------------------------------------------

**Paste this into Cursor (verbatim):**

> You are fixing LeanDojo’s Lean REPL used by Dojo (`Lean4Repl.lean`) to prevent crashes on empty stdin reads.
> 
> **Bug:** The REPL currently does `Json.parse` on an empty string when `stdin.getLine` returns `""` (EOF marker / blank line), causing a fatal Lean error: `failed to parse JSON offset 0: unexpected end of input`.
> 
> **Goal:** Modify the REPL so it never crashes on empty lines / EOF marker. It must:
> 
> 1.  Still print the initial `REPL> {...}` JSON handshake immediately.
> 2.  In the main loop, read commands from stdin.
> 3.  If the read line trims to `""`, DO NOT parse it. Instead `IO.sleep` for a few milliseconds and retry.
> 4.  Only attempt `Json.parse` on a non-empty trimmed line.
> 5.  Do not introduce a deadlock: initial `REPL>` output must occur before blocking on stdin.
> 6.  Add an explicit stdout flush after printing any `REPL>` response so Dojo never misses the prompt.
>     
> 
> **Implementation detail:**
> 
> *   Create a helper `partial def readNonempty (stdin : IO.FS.Stream) : IO String` that loops until it gets a non-empty trimmed string. On empty, do `IO.sleep 5` (milliseconds) then recurse.
> *   Replace the current `stdin.getLine` usage in the command-processing loop with `let line ← readNonempty stdin`.
> *   Keep everything else the same (request parsing, state updates, printing responses).
>     
> 
> **Important reference:** Lean streams can return empty string on EOF marker but later still produce data, so empty must not be treated as termination.
> 
> After patching, rebuild the REPL with `lake build Lean4Repl`.

### The exact Lean snippet to use

When Cursor asks “what code”, give it this exact helper:

```lean
partial def readNonempty (stdin : IO.FS.Stream) : IO String := do
  let s := (← stdin.getLine).trim
  if s == "" then
    -- getLine can return "" for blank lines or EOF markers; neither should crash the REPL.
    IO.sleep 5
    readNonempty stdin
  else
    pure s
```

And then the call site should look like:

```lean
let stdin ← IO.getStdin
-- ...
let line ← readNonempty stdin
-- now safe to parse line as JSON
```

Also add flushing right after printing any `REPL>` line, e.g.:

```lean
(← IO.getStdout).flush
```

(Flush matters because Dojo is waiting on that prompt.)

Why this fix is “the right one”
===============================

Two key facts make this the correct approach:

*   LeanDojo’s REPL protocol expects **line-delimited JSON**.
*   Lean’s own doc for streams says: empty string indicates **EOF marker**, but “EOF does not actually close a stream, so further reads may block and return more data.” [cs.rochester.edu](https://www.cs.rochester.edu/~yzhu104/lean-gccjit/Init/System/IO.html)  
    So treating `""` as fatal is incorrect; the REPL must just wait.

And the upstream LeanDojo issue indicates this is not just your setup — it’s a real bug people hit. [GitHub](https://github.com/lean-dojo/LeanDojo/issues/250)

* * *

Once this is fixed: where we are vs “beating AlphaProof”
========================================================

Right now you’ve built the **skeleton** that AlphaProof-like systems need:

*   task discovery ✅
*   premise extraction + vector retrieval ✅
*   tactic trace logging ✅
*   memory bootstrap ✅
*   policy training loop ✅
*   MCTS loop ✅ (now blocked by Dojo init bug)

That’s huge groundwork — but **performance vs AlphaProof is not meaningful yet** because:

1.  We’re still on **tiny slices** (2 → 8 theorems)
2.  We haven’t enabled the “real” engines that matter in mathlib:
    *   strong tactic portfolios (simp/linarith/nlinarith/ring, etc.)
    *   lemma selection trained on _thousands_ of states
    *   state embedding that actually reflects structure (your delta/DAG idea)
    *   curriculum / self-play style data growth

So: you’re close to a _working research system_, not close to AlphaProof-level _capability_ yet. But once Dojo is stable, we can start the real scaling steps.

* * *

Answering your “revolutionary delta AST/DAG encoder” question
=============================================================

You’re right to keep it in sight. But it is **Phase 2**, not Phase 1.

**Phase 1 (now):** stabilize the interaction loop + scale data.  
Because the delta encoder’s payoff depends on having _lots_ of trajectories/states to learn invariances from.

**Phase 2 (soon after):** replace string-pp embeddings with a structured representation:

*   parse tactic state into:
    *   goal AST
    *   local context binders + types AST
    *   hypotheses dependencies graph
*   represent **delta(state\_t → state\_{t+1})**:
    *   introduced symbols
    *   eliminated goals
    *   rewritten subtrees
    *   changed binder types
*   feed that to retrieval + policy as the “state embedding”.

But again — first we need the REPL loop to stop crashing.

* * *

## 🎉 COMPREHENSIVE REPORT FOR GPT-PRO

### Executive Summary: LeanDojo Compatibility RESOLVED

**The MCTS evaluation pipeline is now working on real mathlib theorems!**

We solved the LeanDojo compatibility issue by:
1. **Using LeanDojo v2.2.0** (not v4.20.0) - installed from GitHub with --ignore-requires-python
2. **Using mathlib4 v4.11.0** which uses **Lean 4.11.0** - below the 4.12.0 threshold where LeanDojo Issue #211 breaks tactic interaction

---

### Results

| Metric | Value |
|--------|-------|
| Tasks Discovered | 50 theorems from Mathlib/Data/Nat/Defs.lean |
| Tasks Evaluated | 10 |
| Tasks Solved | **4 (40%)** |
| Tasks Timeout | 5 |
| Tasks Error | 1 |
| Total LLM Calls | 12 |
| Premises Indexed | 2,033 |

**Solved Theorems:**
✅ Nat.succ_ne_succ: ['simp']
✅ Nat.succ_succ_ne_one: ['simp']
✅ Nat.one_lt_succ_succ: ['simp']
✅ Nat.not_succ_lt_self: ['simp']

All solved with a single simp tactic - demonstrating the base tactic portfolio is effective!

---

### Root Cause Analysis

We discovered **two separate issues**:

1. **REPL Empty Stdin Bug** (GPT-Pro identified)
   - Status: ✅ Fixed in our Lean4Repl.lean with readNonempty helper
   - But this wasn't the blocking issue

2. **LeanDojo Lean 4.12+ Incompatibility** (Our discovery)
   - Status: ✅ Solved by using older LeanDojo + mathlib versions
   - LeanDojo Issue #211: Tactic interaction broken for Lean >= 4.12.0
   - LeanDojo 4.20.0's ExtractData.lean uses APIs that don't exist in Lean < 4.12

**Solution Path:**
LeanDojo 4.20.0 + Lean 4.20.0 → ❌ Issue #211 breaks tactic interaction
LeanDojo 4.20.0 + Lean 4.11.0 → ❌ ExtractData.lean API mismatch
LeanDojo 2.2.0 + Lean 4.11.0 → ✅ WORKS!

| mathlib4 | v4.11.0 | Commit: 20c73142afa995ac9c8fb80a9bb585a55ca38308 |
| Lean | 4.11.0 | Below 4.12.0 threshold |
| Target File | Mathlib/Data/Nat/Defs.lean | (was Basic.lean in later versions) |

- LeanDojo claims support for "Lean 4 v4.3.0-rc2 or later"

- The solution is to use **LeanDojo 2.2.0 + mathlib4 v4.11.0 (Lean 4.11.0)**

This allows us to:
1. ✅ Run task discovery on real mathlib files
2. ✅ Build premise databases and indices
3. ✅ Execute MCTS with LLM-guided tactic search
4. ✅ Solve theorems (40% success rate on initial test!)

Critical correction to lock in now: stop scraping names; use traced data
------------------------------------------------------------------------

Your “unqualified theorem name” failure is exactly why **theorem discovery must come from LeanDojo’s traced objects**, not regex/string scraping.

LeanDojo already exposes:

*   `TracedFile.get_traced_theorems()` returning `TracedTheorem` objects [LeanDojo](https://leandojo.readthedocs.io/en/latest/traced_data.html)
*   each `TracedTheorem` has:
    *   `theorem` (a `Theorem` object),
    *   `get_theorem_statement()`, `get_traced_tactics()`, `has_tactic_proof()`, etc. [LeanDojo](https://leandojo.readthedocs.io/en/latest/traced_data.html)
*   and the `Theorem` object includes `full_name` and `file_path`. [LeanDojo](https://leandojo.readthedocs.io/en/latest/lean.html)

This one change will eliminate a whole class of “Dojo is failing” false alarms.

About your “revolutionary encoder” question
===========================================

Should we start it now?
-----------------------

**Soon, yes — but one prerequisite comes first:** we need a **steady stream of solved traces** so the encoder has something to learn from.

The good news: you’re now at exactly the stage where this becomes realistic, because Dojo+MCTS is producing real trajectories.

### What “delta AST/DAG encoder” should mean (concretely)

Right now you embed **a pretty-printed string**. That’s the weakest link.

A real “delta encoder” for theorem proving should:

1.  **Canonicalize the proof state**
    *   parse goals into a structured form (hypotheses + target)
    *   normalize binder names (so `a b c` doesn’t matter)
    *   keep constants/constructors/structure names
2.  **Represent state as a graph/DAG**
    *   nodes: constants, types, applications, binders, hypotheses
    *   edges: “applies-to”, “has-type”, “depends-on”
3.  **Represent transitions as deltas**
    *   what hypotheses disappeared/appeared?
    *   what did the target rewrite into?
    *   how many goals split/merged?

This is exactly your intelligence thesis: **compression-progress**.  
Better state compression → less search → fewer LLM calls.

### The practical “Batch after Batch 11” approach (laptop-feasible)

You don’t jump straight to a massive GNN.

You do it in 3 escalating layers:

**Layer A: Structured token encoder (fast win)**

*   Use LeanDojo’s goal parsing (or your own minimal parser).
*   Create tokens like:
    *   `GOAL: Nat.succ ?x = Nat.succ ?y`
    *   `HYP: ?h1 : ?x = ?y`
*   Use a local vectorizer:
    *   hashing vectorizer / tf-idf
    *   plus a tiny MLP  
        This alone often beats generic text embeddings for retrieval/policy.

**Layer B: Delta features**

*   For each tactic step, compute:
    *   added/removed hypothesis tokens
    *   added/removed goal head symbols
    *   goal-count delta
*   Train a small model to predict the tactic-template class from (state tokens + delta features).

**Layer C: Graph encoder (the “revolutionary” one)**

*   Build expression DAGs (even approximate ones).
*   Train a contrastive embedding model: “states that want the same tactic/template should cluster”.

We will start Layer A/B right after the bootstrap loop is reliably producing hundreds–thousands of steps.

* * *

“Large math models” / “math-native intelligence” thought experiment
===================================================================

You’re not wrong that “LLM trained on internet text” is a weird starting point for math.

But: training a brand-new foundation model on a laptop is not the leverage point.

The leverage point is:

*   **Verifier feedback (Lean)**
*   **Search + curriculum**
*   **State compression**
*   **Distillation into small policies**

That _is_ the “math-native” path — because it’s grounded in the proof kernel.

If later you want a “native math LM”:

*   don’t train from scratch
*   fine-tune an open model (LoRA) on your solved traces + mined mathlib traces

One important practical note: LeanDojo 2.2.0 docs list Python `< 3.12` as the supported range [LeanDojo](https://leandojo.readthedocs.io/en/stable/getting-started.html) — so if you’re on Python 3.12 and using `--ignore-requires-python`, it can work, but it’s riskier. On your M3 Max, I’d strongly consider pinning a Python 3.11 venv for stability.

* * *

One more thing: your version pinning is _not optional_
------------------------------------------------------

LeanDojo’s repo README says current LeanDojo supports Lean 4 `v4.3.0-rc2` or later [GitHub](https://github.com/lean-dojo/LeanDojo), but Issue #211 documents a real breakage in tactic-running for repos using Lean `>= v4.12.0`. [GitHub](https://github.com/lean-dojo/LeanDojo/issues/211)  
So treat your working matrix as a “known-good toolchain”, and lock it like production.

Batch 12.2 — Macro mining: the next compression primitive beyond templates
==========================================================================

Right now, your “policy” is a **template chooser**. That’s good, but it compresses only _which single tactic to try_.

The next jump in proof efficiency comes from learning **small programs** (tactic sequences) that reliably work on a class of states.

### Cursor prompt

```text
Implement macro mining from solved traces and integrate macros into the base action space.

Create/modify EXACTLY these files:

A) alphaproofpp/memory/macro_miner.py
- Implement:
  - @dataclass(frozen=True) class Macro:
      tactics: tuple[str, ...]     # length 2..4
      count: int                  # occurrences in traces
      solved_count: int           # occurrences ending in solved (should equal count if sourced from solved traces)
  - def mine_macros(traces_jsonl: str, min_count: int = 3, max_len: int = 4) -> list[Macro]
    * Parse ProofTrace JSONL
    * For each solved trace:
        generate all contiguous subsequences of lengths 2..max_len
        count them
    * Keep those with count >= min_count
    * Deterministic order: sort by (-count, tactics)

  - def macro_to_lean_tactic(m: Macro) -> str
    * Convert ("simp","rw [X]","simp") -> "simp; rw [X]; simp"
    * Use "; " separator.

B) alphaproofpp/cli/build_macros_from_traces.py
- CLI: python -m alphaproofpp.cli.build_macros_from_traces
- Args:
  --traces-jsonl (required)
  --out-json (required)
  --min-count (int, default 3)
  --max-len (int, default 4)
- Save macros as JSON list with fields: tactics(list[str]), count

C) alphaproofpp/prover/action_gen.py
- Extend ActionGenConfig:
  - macro_tactics_json: str | None = None
  - macro_prior: float = 0.23
- In build_action_list(...):
  - if macro_tactics_json provided and file exists:
      load macros once (cache in module-level weak cache)
      add each macro tactic string as Action(tactic=..., prior=macro_prior, source="base")
  - dedupe and apply bans as usual

D) tests/test_macro_miner_unit.py
- No network, no Lean.
- Create a fake traces.jsonl with two solved traces and known sequences.
- Ensure macro mining finds expected subsequences and ordering.
- Ensure macro_to_lean_tactic formatting matches expected.

Constraints:
- Typed.
- Deterministic.
- No LeanDojo dependency in tests.
```

### Why this matters

This is literally your intelligence thesis implemented:

*   **learn** → **compress** into programs → **reuse** → future solves cost less compute.

And it’s one of the most laptop-friendly ways to punch above weight.

Ask cursor

They’re _not quite the same category_, so “better/worse” depends on what you mean by “workflow leverage.”

*   **Cursor** = an **AI-first IDE** (VS Code fork) where the agent is _the center of the product_ (multi-agent UI, background agents, cloud handoff).
*   **Claude Code** = an **agentic coding tool that lives in your terminal**, plus a VS Code extension that mirrors that agent flow (with hooks/checkpoints/subagents).
*   **OpenAI Codex (IDE extension)** = an IDE panel **plus a cloud software-engineering agent** you can delegate tasks to (sandboxed, parallelizable, often more “hands-off”).

Below is how I’d compare them in practice.

* * *

The quickest mental model
-------------------------

### Cursor: “agent-first IDE conductor”

Cursor 2.0 is explicitly built around running _multiple agents in parallel_ (up to eight), isolating their changes via git worktrees / remote machines, and letting you pick the best result. [Cursor+1](https://cursor.com/changelog/2-0?utm_source=chatgpt.com)  
It also has CLI agent modes and **cloud handoff** (push an ongoing conversation to a cloud agent and resume later). [Cursor](https://cursor.com/changelog)

### Claude Code: “terminal-native agent + automation hooks”

Anthropic positions Claude Code as a terminal agent, with a native VS Code extension and features for autonomy: **checkpoints/rewind**, **subagents**, **hooks**, **background tasks**. [Anthropic+1](https://www.anthropic.com/news/enabling-claude-code-to-work-more-autonomously)  
The VS Code docs also emphasize MCP server setup + shared settings between CLI and extension. [Claude Code](https://code.claude.com/docs/en/vs-code)

### Codex: “cloud SWE agent + IDE surface”

OpenAI’s Codex is described as a **cloud-based software engineering agent** running each task in an isolated sandbox preloaded with your repo, able to run commands/tests and produce evidence via terminal logs/test outputs. [OpenAI](https://openai.com/index/introducing-codex/)  
The VS Code marketplace listing for the extension also explicitly frames it as: “Use it side-by-side… or delegate larger tasks to the cloud.” [Visual Studio Marketplace](https://marketplace.visualstudio.com/items?itemName=openai.chatgpt)

* * *

Comparison that actually matters day-to-day
-------------------------------------------

### 1) Multi-agent / parallelism

*   **Cursor:** multi-agent is a first-class feature; up to 8 parallel agents, isolated workspaces, plus “agent-first” interface. [Cursor+1](https://cursor.com/changelog/2-0?utm_source=chatgpt.com)
*   **Claude Code:** has _subagents_ and a workflow built around autonomy + checkpoints; not “8 parallel competing agents” as the headline, but it’s strong for _delegation and automation_. [Anthropic](https://www.anthropic.com/news/enabling-claude-code-to-work-more-autonomously)
*   **Codex:** parallelism is baked in at the cloud-task level (“many tasks in parallel”). [OpenAI](https://openai.com/index/introducing-codex/)

**Practical take:**  
If your goal is “spawn 3–8 attempts, pick best diff,” Cursor tends to feel the most natural. If your goal is “spawn several independent cloud tasks on a repo,” Codex is built for that.

* * *

### 2) Control + safety rails for “agent runs commands”

*   **Claude Code** is unusually strong here because it’s explicitly designed around “tools + permissions + hooks.” It even supports lifecycle hooks (pre/post tool use) to enforce formatting, tests, etc. [Anthropic+1](https://www.anthropic.com/news/enabling-claude-code-to-work-more-autonomously)
*   **Cursor** has agent sandboxing controls and cloud handoff, but its main differentiation is the IDE integration and multi-agent orchestration. [Cursor+1](https://cursor.com/changelog)
*   **Codex** has an isolated cloud sandbox per task and a “show your work” style with logs/citations, and can be guided via repo-level instructions like `AGENTS.md`. [OpenAI+1](https://openai.com/index/introducing-codex/)

**Practical take:**  
For “I want to auto-run format/lint/test on every edit” and _tightly govern what happens_, Claude Code is often the most programmable.

* * *

### 3) IDE experience (the “feel”)

*   **Cursor** tends to be the smoothest if you want the agent deeply embedded in editing (because it _is_ the editor). Its plans explicitly mention “Background Agents” and “Maximum context windows.” [Cursor](https://cursor.com/pricing)
*   **Claude Code extension** gives you a dedicated sidebar + inline diffs and is designed for IDE users who still want the Claude Code workflow. [Anthropic+1](https://www.anthropic.com/news/enabling-claude-code-to-work-more-autonomously)
*   **Codex extension** gives you a panel, context from open files/selection, and a path to cloud tasks from inside your IDE. [Visual Studio Marketplace+1](https://marketplace.visualstudio.com/items?itemName=openai.chatgpt)

**Practical take:**  
If you’re already fast in Cursor, you’re not “missing” IDE ergonomics by not using the others—you’d be adding different _agent behaviors_ and model tradeoffs.

* * *

Pricing and access (only what’s supported by official docs)
-----------------------------------------------------------

*   **Claude Code**: available with Claude **Pro** and **Max**; the help center lists Pro at $20/mo, Max tiers at $100 and $200/mo. [Claude Help Center+1](https://support.claude.com/en/articles/11145838-using-claude-code-with-your-pro-or-max-plan)
*   **Cursor**: Pro $20/mo, Pro+ $60/mo, Ultra $200/mo (and Teams $40/user/mo), with features like background agents and higher usage tiers. [Cursor](https://cursor.com/pricing)
*   **Codex**: included with ChatGPT plans; Codex pricing page shows usage windows for _local messages_ and _cloud tasks_ depending on Plus/Pro/Business, and supports API-key usage for local runs. [OpenAI Developers+1](https://developers.openai.com/codex/pricing/)

* * *

So… are Claude Code / Codex “better” than Cursor?
-------------------------------------------------

### When **Claude Code** is better than Cursor

*   You want a **terminal-first agent** that lives with your shell tooling and git habits. [GitHub](https://github.com/anthropics/claude-code)
*   You want **hooks/checkpoints/subagents** as a core workflow primitive (auto-format, auto-test, rewind). [Anthropic+1](https://www.anthropic.com/news/enabling-claude-code-to-work-more-autonomously)
*   You want **MCP-based tool extension** and shared settings across CLI + VS Code. [Claude Code](https://code.claude.com/docs/en/vs-code)

### When **Codex** is better than Cursor

*   You want to **delegate bigger jobs to the cloud** in isolated sandboxes and let them run independently, then review the final patch + logs. [OpenAI+1](https://openai.com/index/introducing-codex/)
*   You want “SWE-agent style” iterations where it runs tests until passing and shows evidence. [OpenAI](https://openai.com/index/introducing-codex/)

### When **Cursor** is better than both

*   You want the **tightest editing loop** (agent + IDE as one thing) and the best “review diffs quickly and keep coding” flow.
*   You want to run **many parallel agents competing** and pick the best output, inside one agent-centric UI. [Cursor+1](https://cursor.com/changelog/2-0?utm_source=chatgpt.com)
*   You want the Cursor ecosystem features like **cloud handoff** and CLI modes that mirror editor behavior. [Cursor](https://cursor.com/changelog)

* * *

For _your_ use case (building Proof‑OS / AlphaProof++ on a laptop)
------------------------------------------------------------------

Given your workflow:

*   heavy iteration
*   lots of scripts / tests / eval loops
*   frequent multi-file refactors
*   tight feedback cycles

I’d do this:

1.  **Keep Cursor as your main cockpit** (fastest “thinking → edit → run” loop; plus multi-agent when you want competing solutions). [Cursor+1](https://cursor.com/changelog/2-0?utm_source=chatgpt.com)
2.  Add **Claude Code CLI** specifically for “automation discipline”:
    *   enforce formatting/tests via hooks
    *   checkpoint/rewind when you let it do scary refactors [Anthropic+1](https://www.anthropic.com/news/enabling-claude-code-to-work-more-autonomously)
3.  Use **Codex** when you have “bigger jobs” that you truly want offloaded to a cloud agent sandbox (esp. if you want logs/evidence and parallel tasks). [OpenAI+1](https://openai.com/index/introducing-codex/)

Your M3 Max mostly helps with **local indexing + running Lean/Python + tests**. The “smartness” is still mostly the hosted model(s), so the tool that best reduces _human overhead_ often wins.

* * *

A simple way to know for sure (1-hour A/B test)
-----------------------------------------------

Pick one real task from your repo, e.g. “add macro-mining + integrate into bootstrap loop” (multi-file, tests).

Run it three times:

*   Cursor Agent / multi-agent
*   Claude Code CLI
*   Codex (IDE extension in agent mode / cloud task)

Score each on:

*   time to passing tests
*   number of back-and-forth turns
*   diff cleanliness
*   how often it got “stuck” on repo context

That experiment will give you a grounded answer faster than any opinion.

* * *

If you tell me which languages you’re coding most right now in this repo (Python + Lean + maybe TS?), I can suggest a “best default split” like: Cursor for edits, Claude Code for refactors/tests, Codex for cloud tasks + PR-style changes.

Batch 13 — Prompt 13.3

Start the delta representation path: canonicalized symbolic text (Stage A)
--------------------------------------------------------------------------

This is the first step toward the “true delta AST/DAG encoder”.

We’ll implement a **canonicalizer** that removes irrelevant surface noise (variable names, whitespace) and preserves the **structural symbols** and **constant identifiers** that matter.

Paste into Cursor:

```text
Implement a canonicalized symbolic representation for Lean text, and use it for embeddings.

Create/modify EXACTLY these files:

A) alphaproofpp/embeddings/canonicalize.py
- Implement:
  - def tokenize_lean(text: str) -> list[str]
    Rules:
      * split into tokens: identifiers OR single non-space characters
      * identifiers match: [A-Za-z_][A-Za-z0-9_'.]*  (keep dots and apostrophes)
      * everything else: each char as token (⊢, :, =, +, *, →, etc.)
  - def canonicalize_lean_text(text: str) -> str
    Rules:
      * trim lines, collapse whitespace
      * tokenize
      * map tokens:
         - if token matches identifier and starts with uppercase letter: KEEP (Nat, List, HAdd, etc)
         - if token matches identifier and starts with lowercase: replace with "v" (variable placeholder)
         - if token is an integer literal: replace with "NUM"
         - keep punctuation/symbol tokens as-is
      * join with single spaces

B) alphaproofpp/embeddings/canonical_embedder.py
- Implement wrapper class CanonicalizingEmbedder:
  - __init__(self, base_embedder)
  - embed_texts(texts: list[str]) -> np.ndarray
    * canonicalize each text then call base_embedder.embed_texts
  - dim property passes through

C) alphaproofpp/premises/build_index.py (or wherever build_premise_index_from_jsonl lives)
- Add option to canonicalize before embedding premises.
- If the builder is a CLI, add a flag:
  --canonicalize
  When set, wrap the embedder with CanonicalizingEmbedder.

D) alphaproofpp/cli/build_premise_index_from_premise_db.py
- Add --canonicalize flag and pass through.

E) alphaproofpp/cli/eval_tasks_mcts.py
- Add --canonicalize flag:
  - If set, use CanonicalizingEmbedder for state embeddings (DeltaBlockStateEmbedder should embed canonicalized pp).
  - IMPORTANT: canonicalization must be consistent between premise index build and eval.
  - If user passes --canonicalize but index was built without it, print a warning.

F) tests/test_canonicalize_unit.py
- Unit tests:
  1) Two pps that differ only in variable names canonicalize to the same string:
     "a b : Nat\n⊢ a = b" and "x y : Nat\n⊢ x = y"
  2) "Nat.succ a = Nat.succ b" keeps Nat.succ and replaces a,b with v.
  3) Integer literals become NUM.

Constraints:
- Typed.
- No LeanDojo in tests.
```

## Response:
Thought for 7m 58s

Yes — **we will swap both**, but **not by jumping straight to a huge transformer**. The plan is: keep the LogReg/heuristics as _baselines + fallbacks_, and introduce **small learned neural models** at the exact points where your Batch 13 metrics show the bottleneck.

What your Batch 13 run proves is huge:

*   **Anchor forcing fixed “wasted compute”**: most solvable-by-`simp` theorems now solve in **1–3 sims** (≈ 1–3 verifier misses).
*   The remaining 13–14 timeouts are now “real” hard cases: they need **the right lemma + the right multi-step plan**, not just “try `simp` early.”

That’s exactly where learned policy/value help.

* * *

Clarify: what “policy” do we currently have?
--------------------------------------------

Right now you basically have **two different “policies”**:

1.  **Template policy** (your current LogReg):  
    Predicts a _tactic template class_ like `simp`, `rw`, `apply`, etc.  
    This is mostly a _cheap prior_.
2.  **Action generator / lemma instantiation** (currently mostly heuristics):  
    Turns retrieval results into concrete actions like:
    *   `apply Nat.succ_injective`
    *   `rw [Nat.add_assoc]`
    *   `simp [Nat.add_comm, ...]`

The **hard part** is (2), not (1).

A template predictor can say “use `rw`”, but the real intelligence is: **which lemma(s), in what order, at what depth**.

So when you ask “swap LogReg for transformer,” the better question is:

> Are we going to replace _lemma-choice + value guidance_ with learned models?

**Yes. That’s the next leap.**

* * *

Are we going to replace the heuristic value function?
-----------------------------------------------------

### Yes — soon.

Your current leaf heuristic is a placeholder. It’s fine for plumbing and for anchor-theorems, but it’s weak for the timeouts.

### What we replace it with (laptop-feasible)

A **tiny neural value network** (MLP) trained from your own search data:

*   **Input:** state embedding (your current 1536‑d vector; later the delta/structured embedding)
*   **Target:** a value signal derived from MCTS / rollouts, e.g.:
    *   “probability of solving within remaining depth/budget”
    *   or “best Q observed from this node”

This is _exactly_ how AlphaZero-style systems work, except we’re doing it in a much more compute-efficient, distillation-friendly way.

And crucially: **MLP value nets work shockingly well** once you have a few thousand state→outcome samples (which you can generate from your own runs).

* * *

Are we going to replace the LogReg template policy?
---------------------------------------------------

### Yes — but not the way you think.

Replacing LogReg with a transformer **is not the first win**.

The first win is learning **lemma selection** (a “pointer” / reranker) — because that’s what actually turns timeouts into solves.

### The likely progression

**Step 1 (next batch):**  
Replace LogReg + heuristics with:

*   **Lemma scorer (neural reranker)**: chooses which premise(s) to try for `apply/exact/rw/simp[...]`
*   **Value net (neural)**: guides MCTS toward branches that are actually promising

Both can be small MLPs trained on CPU/MPS quickly.

**Step 2 (later):**  
If we need more expressiveness, we upgrade the policy from MLP → **small transformer over canonical tokens** (or even graph/DAG).

So yes: transformers can come, but only after we prove the data loop is strong enough to justify them.

* * *

Where does the “new architecture / revolutionary delta AST/DAG encoder” fit?
----------------------------------------------------------------------------

This is the part I _do not_ want to lose.

Swapping LogReg → transformer is mostly “more capacity.”  
The **delta AST/DAG encoder** is “better substrate” — it changes the _problem geometry_.

### Why it’s likely a bigger payoff than “bigger nets”

Right now your embeddings are downstream of pretty-printed pp text. That’s lossy and noisy.

A structural delta encoder does two huge things:

1.  **Generalization:**  
    Two states that are “the same proof situation” but with different variable names or formatting become _the same representation_.
2.  **Compute scaling:**  
    When a tactic changes only a small part of the goal/context, you update only a small part of the embedding (delta), instead of re-embedding the whole thing.

That’s exactly “compression-progress” as engineering.

### Timeline

We’ve already started Stage A (canonicalization).  
Next steps:

*   **Stage B (very soon):** delta features + structural token multiset / bag-of-symbols (cheap, measurable win)
*   **Stage C (after we have more traces):** true expression DAG encoder with caching by node-hash

So yes: this _is coming_, and it is not a “maybe.” It’s one of the main ways a laptop can punch above its compute weight.

* * *

What we should do next (Batch 14 direction)
-------------------------------------------

Your Batch 13 data basically screams the next two modules:

### 1) Lemma-choice learning (premise reranker)

Train a model that learns:

*   given a state embedding and a candidate premise embedding,
*   predict whether that premise will be useful **now** (for apply/rw/simp lemma lists)

This can be done as:

*   **binary classification** (used vs not used)
*   or **pairwise ranking** (used premise should score above unused)

Then your action generator becomes:

*   generate 100 candidate actions cheaply
*   pick the top 20 using the reranker (much better than raw cosine similarity)

This is where timeouts start turning into solves.

### 2) Learned value for MCTS

Replace heuristic leaf value with value net output.

This converts MCTS from “search in the dark” into “search with a compass.”

* * *

Answering your question directly
--------------------------------

### “Are we going to swap heuristics for neural networks?”

**Yes.** Specifically:

*   heuristic value → **value net**
*   hand-coded priors → **learned priors** (from policy/reranker)

But we’ll keep heuristics as:

*   baselines (to measure improvement)
*   fallbacks (when model is uncertain or data is sparse)

### “Is the policy model just LogReg and will we swap it?”

**Yes**, it’s currently a **Logistic Regression** template classifier.  
We will swap it, but:

*   first to a **neural lemma scorer + value net** (bigger win)
*   then maybe to a **small transformer** if the data and bottleneck justify it

### “Do we need a fundamentally different architecture than transformers?”

Possibly — but the first “fundamental” change is **state representation** (delta/DAG), not the policy head.

Transformers can still be the function approximator on top of better representations.

* * *

One more note on your Batch 13 “post-bootstrap got -1 solve”
------------------------------------------------------------

That doesn’t worry me yet. With small sample sizes and a theorem that was “barely solved at sim=80,” tiny changes in action ordering/priors can flip solve/timeout at the boundary.

Once we add:

*   reranker (better ordering)
*   learned value (better exploration)
*   and/or bump sims to 120 for that borderline group

…that instability usually disappears.

## Response:
Thought for 16m 22s

Yes — **your “do both” recommendation is the right move**, and it lines up perfectly with what Batch 13 taught us: we’ve squeezed most of the low‑hanging “anchor tactic” juice, so the next gains require **better lemma choice + better value guidance**, and those need **more diverse tasks + more diverse traces**.

Now to your two questions about model direction:

1) Are we going to swap LogReg/heuristics for neural nets?
==========================================================

**Yes — but in a very specific order.**

### What stays (for now)

*   **LogReg “template policy”** stays as a _cheap, reliable baseline prior_.
*   Simple heuristics stay as _fallbacks_ for when learned models are untrained / uncertain.

### What changes next (starting Batch 14)

The two components that actually turn your **timeouts → solves** are:

1.  **Neural premise reranker (lemma-choice learning)**  
    This is the big one. Template prediction (“use `rw`”) isn’t the bottleneck — **choosing the right lemma(s)** is.
2.  **Neural value model**  
    Replaces the heuristic leaf value so MCTS stops spending budget exploring dead branches. This is how you convert “search” into “guided search.”

So yes: we’re moving from “LogReg/heuristics” → **small neural models**, but we’ll do it in a laptop‑friendly way (small MLPs first, then bigger only if justified).

* * *

2) Are we swapping the policy model to a transformer or new architecture?
=========================================================================

**Not immediately**, and that’s intentional.

A transformer is _capacity_. Right now your limiter is **signal quality + representation**.

The “new architecture” that fits our whole conversation is actually:

*   **better state representation** (canonical + delta + DAG later), and
*   **better feedback loops** (premise‑supervision from LeanDojo annotations + search outcomes)

Once those are in place, we _can_ consider:

*   a small transformer over canonical tokens,
*   or a graph/DAG encoder for Lean expressions.

But if we jump to transformer _before_ we have strong supervision, you’ll just build a bigger black box that still doesn’t know which lemma to pick.

* * *

The key unlock for beating AlphaProof per unit compute
======================================================

Right now you have very few multi-step solved traces, so:

*   macros\_count = 0 (expected)
*   reranker training from your own traces would be too data‑starved

So Batch 14 should **bootstrap data using LeanDojo’s traced tactic annotations**:

LeanDojo’s traced tactics expose:

*   `state_before` and `state_after` (pretty-printed goal states),
*   and `get_annotated_tactic()` which returns the tactic marked with premise links plus provenance info for premises used. [leandojo.readthedocs.io](https://leandojo.readthedocs.io/en/latest/traced_data.html)  
    The LeanDojo user guide explicitly calls out that traced theorems/tactics provide info like premises used. [leandojo.readthedocs.io](https://leandojo.readthedocs.io/en/stable/user-guide.html?utm_source=chatgpt.com)

That is basically “free supervised data” for premise selection — on your laptop.

Batch 14A — Build a 50-task mixed benchmark set with a train/eval split
-----------------------------------------------------------------------

### Goal

*   Collect tasks from **Nat/Defs + Nat/Basic**
*   If still < 50 dojo‑ok, automatically fill from other files under `Mathlib/Data/Nat/`
*   Produce:
    *   `tasks_all.jsonl` (50)
    *   `tasks_train.jsonl` (40)
    *   `tasks_eval.jsonl` (10)
*   Deterministic sampling + split (hash-based)

### Cursor prompt

```text
Implement a taskset builder that produces a deterministic 50-task benchmark with train/eval split.

Create EXACTLY these files:

A) alphaproofpp/tasks/taskset_builder.py
- Implement:
  - def stable_hash(s: str, seed: str) -> str  # sha1 hex
  - def sample_tasks(tasks: list[dict], k: int, seed: str) -> list[dict]
    * Sort by stable_hash(theorem_name, seed) and take first k
  - def split_tasks(tasks: list[dict], train_k: int, seed: str) -> tuple[list[dict], list[dict]]
    * Same hashing order; first train_k are train; rest eval
  - def dedupe_tasks(tasks: list[dict]) -> list[dict]
    * Dedup by theorem_name, keep first occurrence (stable)
  - def group_counts_by_file(tasks: list[dict]) -> dict[str,int]

B) alphaproofpp/cli/build_mathlib_nat_taskset.py
- CLI: python -m alphaproofpp.cli.build_mathlib_nat_taskset
- Args:
  --repo-url (required)
  --commit (required)
  --target-total (default 50)
  --train-count (default 40)
  --seed (default "nat50-v1")
  --dojo-timeout-sec (default 30)
  --out-dir (required)
  --file-path (repeatable)  # allow passing Defs and Basic explicitly
  --fallback-prefix (default "Mathlib/Data/Nat/")  # used if dojo-ok < target-total
- Behavior:
  1) Discover tasks from all provided --file-path using existing discover_tasks_from_repo internals.
  2) Filter dojo-ok using existing filter_tasks_dojo_ok internal function (NOT shelling out).
  3) If still less than target-total:
      - discover tasks across all traced files whose path starts with fallback-prefix
      - filter dojo-ok
      - merge with existing, dedupe
  4) sample to target-total (deterministic)
  5) split into train/eval deterministically
  6) Write:
      out-dir/tasks_all.jsonl
      out-dir/tasks_train.jsonl
      out-dir/tasks_eval.jsonl
      out-dir/manifest.json with counts and file histogram

C) tests/test_taskset_builder_unit.py
- Unit tests for stable_hash/sample/split/dedupe (no LeanDojo).
- Use small toy tasks with theorem_name and file_path.

Constraints:
- Deterministic output.
- No network in tests.
```

Batch 14B — Extract “expert” step dataset from LeanDojo traced tactics (premise supervision)
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------

### Goal

Create a dataset of **(state\_before → tactic + used premises)** from mathlib proofs so we can train lemma-choice models with real supervision.

Why this is massive: `TracedTactic.get_annotated_tactic()` gives premise provenance. [leandojo.readthedocs.io](https://leandojo.readthedocs.io/en/latest/traced_data.html)

### Cursor prompt

```text
Create a dataset extractor that uses LeanDojo traced tactics to output supervised examples for premise selection.

Create EXACTLY these files:

A) alphaproofpp/data/expert_steps.py
- Define @dataclass ExpertStep:
  - theorem_name: str
  - file_path: str
  - tactic_raw: str
  - tactic_annotated: str
  - state_before: str
  - state_after: str
  - premises_full_names: list[str]  # extracted from provenance dicts

- Implement:
  - def extract_expert_steps(traced_theorem, atomic_only: bool) -> list[ExpertStep]
    * For each TracedTactic t in traced_theorem.get_traced_tactics(atomic_only=atomic_only):
      - tactic_raw = t.tactic
      - state_before = t.state_before
      - state_after = t.state_after
      - (annotated, provenances) = t.get_annotated_tactic()
      - premises_full_names = [p["full_name"] for p in provenances if "full_name" in p]
      - return steps

B) alphaproofpp/cli/extract_expert_steps_from_repo.py
- CLI: python -m alphaproofpp.cli.extract_expert_steps_from_repo
- Args:
  --repo-url (required)
  --commit (required)
  --include-prefix (required)  # e.g. Mathlib/Data/Nat/
  --exclude-tasks-jsonl (optional)  # exclude theorem_names (to avoid eval leakage)
  --atomic-only (default False)
  --max-theorems (optional)
  --out-jsonl (required)

- Behavior:
  1) Trace/load repo via LeanDojo.
  2) Iterate traced files with path starting include-prefix
  3) Iterate traced_theorems in those files
  4) Skip private theorems
  5) Skip theorems in exclude list (if provided)
  6) Extract expert steps and write JSONL lines.

C) tests/test_expert_steps_unit.py
- No LeanDojo. Just unit test:
  - parsing of provenance dict list → premises_full_names extraction
  - schema serialization (json dumps loads) for ExpertStep

Constraints:
- Keep extractor robust if provenance dict keys differ; ignore missing keys.
- No network in tests.
```
