vNext Philosophy (the thing that fixes the last rewrite)
========================================================

You’re building a system that must run for days and improve. That means:

1.  **Single source of truth** (data alignment is everything)
2.  **Reproducibility** (every generation can be replayed exactly)
3.  **Hard safety rails** (timeouts, subprocess isolation, graceful shutdown)
4.  **Artifacts first** (every attempt writes a record; everything is inspectable)
5.  **Minimal hidden state** (no “magic” global caches; all caches are explicit dirs)

If you keep those 5 invariants, you’ll avoid 80% of the integration debt.

* * *

Batch 1 — The Spine: “Run one theorem → produce one AttemptRecord”
==================================================================

Goal
----

A single command/function that:

*   starts LeanDojo Dojo for a theorem,
*   applies a list of tactics (1..N),
*   returns a structured result,
*   writes an artifact (`attempt.json`) to disk.

This is the “unit cell” for everything later (search, macros, self-play, learning).

Deliverables
------------

### 1) Core domain objects (just a few)

Keep them tiny and stable:

*   **RepoSpec**: `{repo_url, commit}`
*   **TheoremSpec**: `{repo, file_path, theorem_name}`
*   **AttemptRecord**:
    *   attempt\_id (deterministic)
    *   theorem spec
    *   tactics list
    *   status: `solved | not_solved | error | timeout`
    *   init\_pp (initial goal pretty print)
    *   final\_pp (final goal pretty print or null if solved)
    *   timing fields
    *   error fields: `error_kind`, `error_msg` (and optionally traceback)
    *   stats (optional): steps\_attempted, dojo\_init\_ms, etc.

**Key invariant:** AttemptRecord is the _only_ thing other batches should rely on. Everything else can change.

### 2) A LeanDojo wrapper with a clean contract

Create a `DojoSession` abstraction that hides LeanDojo quirks. It should feel like:

*   `open(theorem_spec) -> initial_state`
*   `pp(state) -> str`
*   `run_tactic(state, tactic) -> (new_state OR finished OR error)`
*   always close cleanly

**Key invariant:** all LeanDojo interaction is behind this wrapper.

### 3) Artifact store

A tiny module that writes:

*   `.cache/proofos/attempts/<attempt_id>/attempt.json`
*   maybe `.cache/proofos/attempts/<attempt_id>/stdout.txt` (optional but useful)

**Atomic writes** only. You will thank yourself later.

### 4) A single CLI entrypoint (optional, but great)

One CLI that maps to the same internal function.

* * *

Design decisions that matter in Batch 1
---------------------------------------

### Attempt ID must be deterministic

Do **not** include timestamps in the ID.  
IDs should be stable so you can de-dupe, cache, and replay.

Example stable key:

```
sha1(repo_url + commit + file_path + theorem_name + "\n".join(tactics))
```

You can still store timestamps in the record.

### Status taxonomy: keep it simple now

*   `solved` (ProofFinished)
*   `not_solved` (tactics ended but goal remains)
*   `error` (Lean error, Dojo init failure, invalid tactic, etc.)
*   `timeout` (Batch 1 can skip timeouts if you want; Batch 3 will harden this)

### Don’t do embeddings / retrieval / LLM in Batch 1

Batch 1 must be _boring_ and stable.

* * *

Testing strategy for Batch 1
----------------------------

You want **unit tests that never call LeanDojo** (fast, deterministic) and **optional integration tests** that do.

### Unit tests (no LeanDojo)

*   hashing/deterministic attempt\_id
*   artifact atomic writes produce valid JSON
*   CLI arg parsing (if CLI exists)
*   attempt record serialization roundtrip

### Integration tests (LeanDojo)

Mark as `pytest -m integration` and only run when you want.

Example integration test:

*   run `hello_world` from `lean4-example`
*   verify status is solved
*   verify init\_pp contains expected goal lines

**Key invariant:** your CI/fast loop shouldn’t depend on Lean.

* * *

Batch 1 “success criteria”
--------------------------

You know Batch 1 is done when:

*   you can prove `hello_world` end-to-end
*   you can intentionally fail a tactic and get a clean `error` AttemptRecord
*   the attempt artifact is written reliably
*   the LeanDojo wrapper never leaks state or hangs in common cases

* * *

Batch 2 — UnifiedTheoremDB (single source of truth)
===================================================

This is the thing that prevented compounding before.

Goal
----

Create **one database** (JSONL) that contains:

*   theorem spec (repo, commit, file path, name)
*   dojo-ok boolean
*   optional proof steps (if you extract them)
*   metadata (proof length, templates, heavy score)

And make it the ONLY source for:

*   “full theorem” tasks
*   “subgoal tasks” (prefix replay)

**No more separate “task pool” vs “proof corpus.”**

Key invariant
-------------

If the generator samples something, it must be:

*   dojo-startable **and**
*   if prefix replay is requested, proof steps must exist **in the same record**.

If not, resample. Never “best effort” this.

* * *

Batch 3 — Reproducibility: Generation Plans + Worker Hard Timeouts
==================================================================

Goal
----

Every generation writes a `plan.jsonl` (the exact tasks chosen), and you can replay it later.

This makes debugging scientific.

Also introduce:

*   subprocess isolation per attempt (so no single hang kills the run)
*   hard timeouts (terminate process)

* * *

Batch 4 — Baseline Solver (no ML): Anchors + tiny action set
============================================================

Before MCTS/LLM/retrieval, you want a reliable baseline:

*   try `simp`, `rfl`, `aesop`, `omega`, `linarith` (tiny curated set)
*   stop if solved
*   record which tactic solved

This feeds macro compilation later and gives you a sanity baseline.

* * *

Batch 5–8 (preview, so you see the full arc)
============================================

### Batch 5: Embeddings + Retrieval

OpenAI embeddings are totally fine early — don’t let “local embedder purity” block progress.

### Batch 6: LLM tactic proposer + caching + gating

LLM becomes expensive “oracle,” not default.

### Batch 7: Macro library as fast-path

Macros don’t just exist — they **skip search**.

### Batch 8: Self-play runner + curriculum + observability

Now the system compounds.

* * *

Practical Python guidance (since you’re coming from Java)
=========================================================

You only need a few Python “concepts” to be productive here:

*   **dataclasses** = Java POJOs + builders vibe
*   **typing** = sanity + editor autocomplete
*   **context managers (`with`)** = try-with-resources (perfect for Dojo sessions)
*   **pathlib** = cleaner file paths than string concat
*   **jsonlines** = append-only logs without locking headaches

If you stick to those, you’ll write clean Python without “Python tricks.”

* * *

One strong recommendation for monolith structure
================================================

Even in a monolith, keep _logical_ boundaries:

*   `domain/` (dataclasses: RepoSpec, TheoremSpec, AttemptRecord)
*   `lean/` (DojoSession wrapper)
*   `io/` (ArtifactStore, atomic writes)
*   `cli/` (optional)
*   later: `selfplay/`, `macros/`, `learn/`

Monolith ≠ single file. Monolith = single deployable repo with coherent modules.

* * *

Batch 1 Goal
------------

A boring, rock-solid “unit cell”:

Single-file layout (copy this as a mental template)
===================================================

Create `proofos.py` with these sections:

### 0) Imports + Version

### 5) Attempt Runner (pure orchestration)

### 6) Artifact Store

*   should be the **only place** touching filesystem layout for attempts

### 7) CLI

*   parse args
*   call runner
*   write artifact
*   print summary

### 8) Main guard

*   `if __name__ == "__main__": main()`

The 5 invariants for Batch 1
============================

### Invariant 1: Attempt ID is deterministic

**Do not** include timestamp in the ID.

Stable ID should be derived only from:

*   repo\_url, commit, file\_path, theorem\_name
*   tactics list (exact text)

Why: caching + dedupe + replay later.

### Invariant 2: One attempt produces one JSON artifact

Always write:

`.cache/proofos/attempts/<attempt_id>/attempt.json`

Even on errors. Even if dojo init fails.  
This is how you debug without guessing.

### Invariant 3: Atomic writes only

Write to temp file then `os.replace()`.

This is what makes 10-day runs safe later.

### Invariant 4: LeanDojo is behind a wrapper

No other code touches LeanDojo directly.

Later, when versions change (Lean 4.11 vs 4.20 headaches), you only patch one place.

### Invariant 5: Status taxonomy is small and stable

Use just:

*   `solved`
*   `not_solved`
*   `error`

(Timeouts come in Batch 3 when you add subprocess isolation.)

* * *

What fields your AttemptRecord MUST include (future-proof)
==========================================================

Minimum fields you’ll thank yourself for later:

*   `attempt_id`
*   `theorem` (repo\_url, commit, file\_path, theorem\_name)
*   `tactics` (list)
*   `status`
*   `error_kind` (e.g., `dojo_init`, `lean_error`, `tactic_error`, `unknown`)
*   `error_msg` (string)
*   `started_ts_unix`, `finished_ts_unix`
*   `init_pp`
*   `final_pp` (null if solved)

Optional (but useful even now):

*   `steps_attempted` (how many tactics were applied)
*   `lean_dojo_version` (string)
*   `toolchain_hint` (string)

* * *

Batch 1 “Definition of Done” checklist
======================================

You’re done when you can run three scenarios:

1) Success case (hello\_world)
------------------------------

*   status = `solved`
*   init\_pp has the goal
*   final\_pp is null
*   attempt.json exists

2) “Not solved” case
--------------------

Use a harmless tactic that changes nothing or fails to close goal.

*   status = `not_solved`
*   final\_pp exists and shows remaining goal

3) Error case
-------------

Use an invalid tactic string.

*   status = `error`
*   error\_kind = `lean_error` or `tactic_error`
*   error\_msg captured
*   artifact still written

If those three work reliably, you’ve built the spine.

* * *

============================================

Even if you keep one file, I _strongly_ recommend **at least one separate tests file**.

That gives you sanity without a test harness.

Decision: go **flat-first JSON** (with intentional redundancy)
--------------------------------------------------------------

If the question is “flat vs nested schema,” the answer for _best shot at AlphaProof_ is:

### ✅ Flat-first (plus a tiny bit of structured nesting only where it truly helps)

Why?

*   Self-play produces **millions** of attempt records eventually.  
    Flat JSON is easiest to:
    *   stream to JSONL,
    *   grep/debug,
    *   build datasets from,
    *   index quickly,
    *   compare across runs/generations.
*   Nested schemas look “nice” but become painful for:
    *   analysis,
    *   backwards compatibility,
    *   accidental deep shape changes.

**Key principle:**

> Never rename fields. Never change meanings. Only add fields.

That one rule saves you from weeks of “integration complexity” later.

AttemptRecord v1 schema you should implement in Batch 1
-------------------------------------------------------

Even if you’re only proving `hello_world` right now, bake in the fields that make self-play possible later. You don’t have to _use_ them yet — just include them.

### Why these matter

*   `run_id/gen/mode` is what turns logs into **learning data**.
*   `config_hash` is what lets you answer “why did Gen 0 work but Gen 1 fail?” in 10 seconds.
*   `solved_by` is how you avoid fooling yourself once teacher/macro modes exist.

Batch 2 North Star
==================

Batch 2 = **turn your prover into an agent**:

1.  Observe current Lean goal state
2.  Propose _multiple_ candidate tactics (cheaply)
3.  Execute them (verifier = Lean)
4.  Log _everything_ so you can train later

This is the moment your system stops being “replay” and becomes “search”.

* * *

The single most important design choice in Batch 2
==================================================

**Do NOT structure the LLM call as “write any Lean code you want”.**

Instead, structure it as:

> **Pick from a constrained action grammar**  
> (with optional lemma references restricted to a provided list)

That one move is the difference between:

*   “LLM hallucinates tactic names / lemmas / syntax”  
    vs
*   “LLM is a policy over a _controlled_ action space”

This is how you get reliability + scale.

* * *

1) How should we structure the LLM call for tactic generation?
==============================================================

Recommended interface (mentally)
--------------------------------

Think of your LLM as returning:

*   **N candidates** (not 1)
*   Each candidate has:
    *   action type (simp/rw/apply/exact/intro/cases/induction/aesop/omega/linarith/…)
    *   optional lemma indices (not names)
    *   a confidence score

Then your loop:

1.  Tries anchors first (no LLM call if solved)
2.  Calls LLM once → gets N candidates
3.  Runs candidates in order until one advances the state or solves
4.  If all fail: either expand retrieval (later batch) or do 1 repair call

Why N candidates per call matters:

*   Lean proving is _verifier-cheap_, LLM-calls-expensive.
*   You want **one LLM call → many verifier attempts**.

* * *

2) What prompt format works best for getting Lean tactics from an LLM?
======================================================================

The principle
-------------

**Make the model fill a form, not write prose.**

With OpenAI you can force strict JSON schema via Structured Outputs (`response_format` with JSON schema). This is exactly what it’s for. [OpenAI Platform+1](https://platform.openai.com/docs/guides/structured-outputs)

### Best practice prompt structure

**System message** (stable, cached, reused):

*   Role: “Lean 4 tactic suggestion engine”
*   Rules:
    *   Output MUST match schema
    *   Must not invent lemma names
    *   Only use allowed tactic kinds
    *   No explanations

**User message** (variable per state):

*   The current pretty-printed goal (`state.pp`)
*   A numbered list of _allowed lemmas_ (if available; if not, keep empty)
*   A short list of “recent failures” (optional but strong)
*   Constraints like max length, disallow `admit`, etc.

### Use lemma _indices_, not lemma names

Instead of letting the model output:

```lean
rw [Nat.add_assoc, Nat.add_comm]
```

have it output:

```json
{"kind":"rw","lemma_ids":[12,41]}
```

and you compile that into the Lean tactic string yourself.

This single trick eliminates:

*   typos
*   wrong namespaces
*   hallucinated lemmas

…and turns lemma selection into a _classification problem_.

* * *

3) How do we handle LLM failures gracefully?
============================================

You need a **failure ladder** that guarantees forward motion and stable logging.

Failure ladder (Batch 2)
------------------------

**Step 0 — Anchors (no LLM):**  
Always try in this order (tunable):

1.  `simp`
2.  `rfl`
3.  `aesop?` (careful: can be slow)
4.  `omega` (when Nat/arithmetic looking)

This is exactly the kind of “anchor forcing” that gave huge efficiency in your earlier system.

**Step 1 — LLM propose N candidates (single call):**

*   If JSON invalid (shouldn’t happen with strict schema, but still guard): retry once with “output must be valid JSON”.

**Step 2 — Execute candidates:**  
For each candidate:

*   run tactic
*   if `solved`: done
*   if `ok` and state changes: accept and continue
*   if `error`: log and try next

**Step 3 — One repair call (optional):**  
If all candidates fail, do ONE “repair” call:

*   Provide: state.pp + the top 3 failing tactics + Lean error messages
*   Ask for N new candidates

Then stop. Don’t loop repairs forever (it kills throughput).

Logging requirement (non-negotiable)
------------------------------------

Every LLM call should write an artifact:

*   prompt hash
*   raw response
*   parsed candidates
*   model/provider
*   latency
*   token usage (if available)

Because later: policy/value training lives or dies on trace quality.

Right now your `AttemptRecord` captures only the initial state and final outcome

tlq0

.  
For Batch 2, add a **StepRecord list** (even if you keep it simple).

Minimum StepRecord fields:

*   `i` (step index)
*   `state_before_pp`
*   `tactic`
*   `result` (“ok/solved/error”)
*   `state_after_pp` (if ok)
*   `error` (if error)

This will pay off massively later (macros, imitation learning, value training).

* * *

4) Should we use OpenAI, Claude, or a local model?
==================================================

My aggressive recommendation: start with OpenAI for Batch 2
-----------------------------------------------------------

Reason: **Structured outputs + prompt caching** give you higher reliability per dollar and higher throughput per unit effort.

*   Structured outputs (strict JSON schema) is explicitly supported in the API docs and designed for “model output must match this schema.” [OpenAI Platform+1](https://platform.openai.com/docs/guides/structured-outputs)
*   Prompt caching can reduce latency and cost when your system prompt stays stable across calls (which it will). [OpenAI Platform+1](https://platform.openai.com/docs/guides/prompt-caching)
*   The Responses API is positioned as the “most advanced interface” in the API reference. [OpenAI Platform](https://platform.openai.com/docs/api-reference/responses)

But architect for swap
----------------------

Even in a monolith file, make a tiny provider interface:

*   `call_llm(messages, schema) -> parsed_json`
*   implementation 1: OpenAI
*   implementation 2: Claude (later)
*   implementation 3: local llama.cpp (later)

Then you can benchmark providers on _your_ states:

*   “successfully produces syntactically valid tactic”
*   “actually advances Lean state”
*   $/proof-step

Local model reality check (still aggressive, still honest)
----------------------------------------------------------

Local models are awesome for **cheap breadth** (lots of attempts), but the minute you want:

*   correct Lean syntax
*   correct lemma selection
*   multi-step proof planning

…quality matters more than raw tokens.

So: start with OpenAI to get the pipeline working and data accumulating. Then later you can distill into a local scorer/policy.

* * *

5) Other Batch 2 questions you didn’t ask but matter
====================================================

A) What should the LLM output: raw tactic strings vs typed actions?
-------------------------------------------------------------------

**Typed actions win.**  
Raw strings are fragile. Typed actions let _you_ generate canonical tactic strings.

Example action schema (conceptual):

*   `kind`: `"simp" | "rw" | "apply" | "exact" | "intro" | "cases" | "induction" | ...`
*   `lemma_ids`: list\[int\]
*   `args`: optional small strings (like variable name for `cases x`)
*   `confidence`: float 0..1

B) How do you stop the model from “cheating” (admit/sorry)?
-----------------------------------------------------------

Add explicit disallow list in system prompt AND runtime filter:

*   reject tactics containing: `admit`, `sorry`, `by_contra`, etc (your choice)

C) Determinism & reproducibility
--------------------------------

For research velocity:

*   keep temperature low (0–0.3) initially
*   store model name + settings in every attempt artifact

D) Cost control without becoming timid
--------------------------------------

Do **gating**:

*   If anchor tactics solve it → 0 LLM calls
*   If state hash already seen with known best tactic → replay it
*   Only call LLM when state is novel or stuck

This is how you get “AlphaZero-ish efficiency” even before full self-play.

Batch 2 Deliverables (what you should implement next)
=====================================================

Since you’re coding it yourself, here’s the clean checklist:

1.  **Step-level trace logging**
    *   Add `steps: list[StepRecord]` to AttemptRecord
    *   Write every tactic attempt result
2.  **LLM proposer**
    *   `propose_tactics(state_pp, allowed_lemmas) -> list[Candidate]`
    *   Force structured output (JSON schema)
3.  **Candidate execution loop**
    *   `solve(theorem_spec)`: anchors → LLM propose N → execute → optional repair → stop
4.  **LLM caching**
    *   key by `(model, system_prompt_hash, user_prompt_hash)`
    *   store raw response + parsed candidates
5.  **Failure ladder**
    *   Always terminates
    *   Always logs
